========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Well, doctor, I... I suppose I came in because things have just been so off lately, especially with my eating. It's been going on for months now, this whole ordeal with food. It's like my stomach and my brain aren't even talking to each other anymore. Some days, I wake up and the very thought of food is just... it's too much. I'll spend the entire day just picking at a piece of dry toast, maybe nursing a cup of black coffee until it goes cold, and that's it. That's all I can manage. But then, out of nowhere, it swings the other way. I'll find myself wandering the aisles of the grocery store, and I'll just stand there in the bakery section, staring at all the cakes and cookies. And I buy them. I buy so many sweets, things I know I shouldn't even be eating, things I don't even really enjoy that much. I'll sit at my kitchen table and just eat them, one after another, and I don't even taste them half the time. It's just this... this compulsion, I guess you could call it. And it's so frustrating because I've always been fairly regular with my meals, you know? Breakfast, lunch, dinner, maybe a little snack in the afternoon. But now, it's just this chaotic mess, and I don't know how to fix it. It's like I have no control over my own body anymore.

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
No, no, it's not like that at all. I mean, I'm not sitting here counting calories or obsessing over the scale, you know? I've never been one of those women who stands in front of the mirror pinching every little bit of flesh, worrying about fitting into a certain dress size. I'm 50 years old, for heaven's sake, I've lived in this body long enough to just let it be. And I'm certainly not starving myself on purpose. When I don't eat, when I just sit there with that dry toast I mentioned, it's not because I'm trying to lose weight or because I think I'm too heavy. It's just... the desire is gone. The physical sensation of hunger just completely vanishes, like someone flipped a switch in my brain and turned off the part that tells me I need sustenance. And then, when I do eat all those sweets, it's not a failure of some diet I'm on. It's just this strange, hollow feeling I'm trying to fill, even though I know a dozen cookies won't actually do it. So, no, I wouldn't say I'm concerned about my shape, not in the way you're asking. It's more about how disconnected I feel from the whole process of nourishing myself. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Oh, yes. Yes, I have. It's actually funny you ask that, because my husband was just saying the other day that my sewing room has been awfully quiet lately. I used to... well, I used to get so excited about my quilting squares. I have these beautiful floral prints I bought on sale down at the fabric store, and I had this whole pattern mapped out for a double-wedding ring quilt. I used to lay them all out on the spare bed and just feel so much joy figuring out the color blocks and the borders. It would take up my whole afternoon. But now? I walk past that room, I look at all those supplies neatly stacked in their little clear plastic bins, and I just... I feel absolutely nothing. I can't make myself care the way I used to. 

And it's not just the quilting, doctor. It's really everything. Tuesday nights have been bowling night for me and a few of the ladies from the neighborhood for, oh, going on seven years now. We even had these silly matching shirts made a few years back. I used to look forward to it all week, just the chatter and the sound of the pins and getting out of the house, but the last few months... I just can't bring myself to go. They call and call, and I just sit there and stare at the phone ringing. I don't even care about my average slipping. 

And the church, too. I've always been the one in charge of planning the potlucks for the fellowship hall. You know how it is, if you don't organize it properly and stay on top of people, you end up with six macaroni salads, a dozen store-bought dinner rolls, and not a single hot main dish. I used to love organizing those sign-up sheets, calling people up, making sure everything was perfect and welcoming for the congregation. I took pride in it. But lately, I look at the church calendar hanging on my fridge, and it's just... it's just a piece of paper. The dates are coming up and I just can't muster up an ounce of enthusiasm to pick up the phone or plan a menu. It's like all the color has just drained right out of my life, and all these things that used to fill up my days and make me happy just seem like... like mountains I can't climb. I just don't care anymore, and I don't understand where all that energy went.

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Well, yes, I suppose I have. It's... it's like this constant weight, doctor. Every single morning, I wake up and before my feet even hit the floor, before I've even had a chance to think about what day it is or what I'm supposed to be doing, there's just this... this heavy, sad feeling sitting right on my chest. It's almost physical, the way it presses down on me. I'll shuffle out to the kitchen, and my kitchen, it's this lovely bright yellow, you see. My husband painted it for me a few years ago because he knows how much I love the morning sun coming through the window over the sink. And I'll get the coffee going, just like I always do, listening to the pot gurgle and spit, but it doesn't matter. I can be standing right there in the sunlight, holding a warm mug, and I just feel like all the warmth has gone out of the world. It's just cold and gray inside my head. 

And there's no reason for it! That's the part that really gets to me, doctor. I have a good life. A wonderful husband, a nice home, we're not struggling to pay the bills or anything like that. I shouldn't be feeling this way. But most days, it's just there, this deep, unshakeable sadness that follows me from room to room. I'll sit in my armchair in the living room, looking out the window at the neighbor's garden, and I'll just start crying. For absolutely no reason at all. The tears just come, and I can't stop them, and I sit there wiping my face with a tissue, feeling so foolish because nothing bad has happened. It's just this pervasive gloom, this feeling that everything is just... sad. And it doesn't lift. It just lingers there, day in and day out, making everything feel so incredibly difficult and pointless. I just don't know how to shake it off, doctor. It's like I'm stuck under a dark cloud that won't move, no matter how hard the wind blows.

I've never been one of those women who stands in front of the mirror pinching every little bit of flesh, worrying about fitting into a certain dress size. I'm 50 years old, for heaven's sake, I've lived in this body long enough to just let it be. And I'm certainly not starving myself on purpose. When I don't eat, when I just sit there with that dry toast I mentioned, it's not because I'm trying to lose weight or because I think I'm too heavy. It's just... the desire is gone. The physical sensation of hunger just completely vanishes, like someone flipped a switch in my brain and turned off the part that tells me I need sustenance. And then, when I do eat all those sweets, it's not a failure of some diet I'm on. It's just this strange, hollow feeling I'm trying to fill, even though I know a dozen cookies won't actually do it. So, no, I wouldn't say I'm concerned about my shape, not in the way you're asking. It's more about how disconnected I feel from the whole process of nourishing myself. It's just so bizarre, doctor, to have your body do these things without any real reason or plan behind it.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed that you don't enjoy things as much as you used to, or that you've lost interest in activities you once found pleasurable?\n\nPatient: Oh, yes. Yes, I have. It's actually funny you ask that, because my husband was just saying the other day that my sewing room has been awfully quiet lately. I used to... well, I used to get so excited about my quilting squares. I have these beautiful floral prints I bought on sale down at the fabric store, and I had this whole pattern mapped out for a double-wedding ring quilt. I used to lay them all out on the spare bed and just feel so much joy figuring out the color blocks and the borders. It would take up my whole afternoon. But now? I walk past that room, I look at all those supplies neatly stacked in their little clear plastic bins, and I just... I feel absolutely nothing. I can't make myself care the way I used to. \n\nAnd it's not just the quilting, doctor. It's really everything. Tuesday nights have been bowling night for me and a few of the ladies from the neighborhood for, oh, going on seven years now. We even had these silly matching shirts made a few years back. I used to look forward to it all week, just the chatter and the sound of the pins and getting out of the house, but the last few months... I just can't bring myself to go. They call and call, and I just sit there and stare at the phone ringing. I don't even care about my average slipping. \n\nAnd the church, too. I've always been the one in charge of planning the potlucks for the fellowship hall. You know how it is, if you don't organize it properly and stay on top of people, you end up with six macaroni salads, a dozen store-bought dinner rolls, and not a single hot main dish. I used to love organizing those sign-up sheets, calling people up, making sure everything was perfect and welcoming for the congregation. I took pride in it. But lately, I look at the church calendar hanging on my fridge, and it's just... it's just a piece of paper. The dates are coming up and I just can't muster up an ounce of enthusiasm to pick up the phone or plan a menu. It's like all the color has just drained right out of my life, and all these things that used to fill up my days and make me happy just seem like... like mountains I can't climb. I just don't care anymore, and I don't understand where all that energy went.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling down or sad most of the time, even when there's no particular reason for it?\n\nPatient: Well, yes, I suppose I have. It's... it's like this constant weight, doctor. Every single morning, I wake up and before my feet even hit the floor, before I've even had a chance to think about what day it is or what I'm supposed to be doing, there's just this... this heavy, sad feeling sitting right on my chest. It's almost physical, the way it presses down on me. I'll shuffle out to the kitchen, and my kitchen, it's this lovely bright yellow, you see. My husband painted it for me a few years ago because he knows how much I love the morning sun coming through the window over the sink. And I'll get the coffee going, just like I always do, listening to the pot gurgle and spit, but it doesn't matter. I can be standing right there in the sunlight, holding a warm mug, and I just feel like all the warmth has gone out of the world. It's just cold and gray inside my head. \n\nAnd there's no reason for it! That's the part that really gets to me, doctor. I have a good life. A wonderful husband, a nice home, we're not struggling to pay the bills or anything like that. I shouldn't be feeling this way. But most days, it's just there, this deep, unshakeable sadness that follows me from room to room. I'll sit in my armchair in the living room, looking out the window at the neighbor's garden, and I'll just start crying. For absolutely no reason at all. The tears just come, and I can't stop them, and I sit there wiping my face with a tissue, feeling so foolish because nothing bad has happened. It's just this pervasive gloom, this feeling that everything is just... sad. And it doesn't lift. It just lingers there, day in and day out, making everything feel so incredibly difficult and pointless. I just don't know how to shake it off, doctor. It's like I'm stuck under a dark cloud that won't move, no matter how hard the wind blows.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. If it did not change, leave \"note\" empty.\n- If the candidates have narrowed to one and you are confident enough that no further questioning is needed, return true for is_final.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"candidates\": [\"<ICD-10 code>\", \"...\"], \"note\": \"<why the list changed, or empty>\", \"is_final\": <true or false>}\n"
  },

    "content": "Return the JSON now."
  }

I can be standing right there in the sunlight, holding a warm mug, and I just feel like all the warmth has gone out of the world. It's just cold and gray inside my head. \n\nAnd there's no reason for it! That's the part that really gets to me, doctor. I have a good life. A wonderful husband, a nice home, we're not struggling to pay the bills or anything like that. I shouldn't be feeling this way. But most days, it's just there, this deep, unshakeable sadness that follows me from room to room. I'll sit in my armchair in the living room, looking out the window at the neighbor's garden, and I'll just start crying. For absolutely no reason at all. The tears just come, and I can't stop them, and I sit there wiping my face with a tissue, feeling so foolish because nothing bad has happened. It's just this pervasive gloom, this feeling that everything is just... sad. And it doesn't lift. It just lingers there, day in and day out, making everything feel so incredibly difficult and pointless. I just don't know how to shake it off, doctor. It's like I'm stuck under a dark cloud that won't move, no matter how hard the wind blows.\n\n[Last Question]\nHave you noticed any significant changes in your appetite or weight lately, like losing or gaining a lot without trying?\n\n[Patient Info]\n- demographics: Female / 50 / not_in_workforce / high_school\n- severity: Mild\n\n[Targeted Info]\nname: Weight_Appetite_Change\nmanifestation: \"I came in because my eating has gotten all turned around for months now, where some days I pick at toast and coffee and other days I stand in the bakery aisle buying sweets I don't even really enjoy.\"\n\n[Conversation Style]\nYou are a Verbose patient.\nA verbose patient may\n1) provide excessively detailed responses when describing their symptoms and experiences, even when the details are directly relevant,\n2) elaborate extensively on the background, events, thoughts, feelings, and personal experiences surrounding their symptoms, and\n3) demonstrate difficulty allowing the doctor to guide the interview or move on to another clinical topic.\nThis is how you are, not a performance you put on in every reply — let it show as much or as little as the moment calls for.\n\nWhat to do:\n- Answer the doctor's last question with what [Targeted Info] holds, in your own words.\n- You are speaking out loud, not reading a line you prepared. Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Well, like I was trying to explain a little bit earlier with the toast and the sweets, my eating has just gotten completely, entirely turned around. In fact, that's really one of the main things that made me realize I needed to come in and see you, because it's been going on for months now and I just can't seem to get a handle on it. 

It's just so unpredictable. There are days, sometimes three or four days in a row, where I'll get up and the thought of putting a real meal together is just... well, it's impossible. I'll pour my coffee, and I'll put a single slice of plain white bread in the toaster, and I'll just sit at the kitchen table picking at it. I'll tear off a little corner, take a sip of coffee, and before I know it, it's three o'clock in the afternoon and that same piece of toast is still sitting there on my napkin, hard as a rock. I just have absolutely no desire for it. 

But then, the very next week, it will swing in the exact opposite direction. I'll be doing my regular shopping down at the grocery store, and I'll just find myself standing there in the middle of the bakery aisle. I'll be staring at all those plastic containers on the tables—the muffins, the brownies, those soft sugar cookies with the thick layer of bright pink frosting and the little round sprinkles. And I'll buy them. I'll buy two or three packages at a time, bring them home, and just eat them one after another. And the most bizarre part of it all, doctor, is that I don't even really enjoy them! They just taste like pure sugar and shortening, not like a nice homemade treat at all, but I just keep eating them anyway. 

So with all that back and forth, you can imagine what a mess it is. My appetite is just all over the place, and because of it, my clothes are fitting so strangely. One week the waistband on my favorite slacks is hanging loose, and the next week it's pinching me because of all those bakery cookies. It's just exhausting, having my body's routine so completely turned upside down for months on end like this.

It's been going on for months now, this whole ordeal with food. It's like my stomach and my brain aren't even talking to each other anymore. Some days, I wake up and the very thought of food is just... it's too much. I'll spend the entire day just picking at a piece of dry toast, maybe nursing a cup of black coffee until it goes cold, and that's it. That's all I can manage. But then, out of nowhere, it swings the other way. I'll find myself wandering the aisles of the grocery store, and I'll just stand there in the bakery section, staring at all the cakes and cookies. But now, it's just this chaotic mess, and I don't know how to fix it. It's like I have no control over my own body anymore.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself overly concerned about your weight or body shape, and have you been intentionally restricting the amount of food you eat on a regular basis?\n\nPatient: No, no, it's not like that at all. I mean, I'm not sitting here counting calories or obsessing over the scale, you know? I've never been one of those women who stands in front of the mirror pinching every little bit of flesh, worrying about fitting into a certain dress size. I'm 50 years old, for heaven's sake, I've lived in this body long enough to just let it be. And I'm certainly not starving myself on purpose. When I don't eat, when I just sit there with that dry toast I mentioned, it's not because I'm trying to lose weight or because I think I'm too heavy. It's just... the desire is gone. The physical sensation of hunger just completely vanishes, like someone flipped a switch in my brain and turned off the part that tells me I need sustenance. And then, when I do eat all those sweets, it's not a failure of some diet I'm on. It's just this strange, hollow feeling I'm trying to fill, even though I know a dozen cookies won't actually do it. So, no, I wouldn't say I'm concerned about my shape, not in the way you're asking. It's more about how disconnected I feel from the whole process of nourishing myself. It's just so bizarre, doctor, to have your body do these things without any real reason or plan behind it.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed that you don't enjoy things as much as you used to, or that you've lost interest in activities you once found pleasurable?\n\nPatient: Oh, yes. Yes, I have. It's actually funny you ask that, because my husband was just saying the other day that my sewing room has been awfully quiet lately. I used to... well, I used to get so excited about my quilting squares. I have these beautiful floral prints I bought on sale down at the fabric store, and I had this whole pattern mapped out for a double-wedding ring quilt. I used to lay them all out on the spare bed and just feel so much joy figuring out the color blocks and the borders. It would take up my whole afternoon. But now? I walk past that room, I look at all those supplies neatly stacked in their little clear plastic bins, and I just... I feel absolutely nothing. I can't make myself care the way I used to. \n\nAnd it's not just the quilting, doctor. It's really everything. Tuesday nights have been bowling night for me and a few of the ladies from the neighborhood for, oh, going on seven years now. We even had these silly matching shirts made a few years back. I used to look forward to it all week, just the chatter and the sound of the pins and getting out of the house, but the last few months... I just can't bring myself to go. They call and call, and I just sit there and stare at the phone ringing. I don't even care about my average slipping. \n\nAnd the church, too. I've always been the one in charge of planning the potlucks for the fellowship hall. You know how it is, if you don't organize it properly and stay on top of people, you end up with six macaroni salads, a dozen store-bought dinner rolls, and not a single hot main dish. I used to love organizing those sign-up sheets, calling people up, making sure everything was perfect and welcoming for the congregation. I took pride in it. But lately, I look at the church calendar hanging on my fridge, and it's just... it's just a piece of paper. My husband painted it for me a few years ago because he knows how much I love the morning sun coming through the window over the sink. And I'll get the coffee going, just like I always do, listening to the pot gurgle and spit, but it doesn't matter. I can be standing right there in the sunlight, holding a warm mug, and I just feel like all the warmth has gone out of the world. spell after my aunt moved in, I never heard voices in the kitchen or believed the whole town was judging me like this. Those experiences show up when I’m feeling sunk and overwhelmed, not during better stretches of my life.\n- key: functional_impairment\n  name: Functional Impairment\n  manifestation: I still get through the basics for my aunt, but I’ve missed church planning calls, put off fair projects, and need neighbors to cover things I used to handle easily. It’s made caregiving and staying involved in town feel much harder than before.\n- key: duration\n  name: Duration\n  description: 7 months\n  manifestation: This has been going on for about seven months, since my aunt came to live with me after her fall.\n\n[Patient Info]\n- demographics: Female / 50 / not_in_workforce / high_school\n- chief_complaint_symptom_code: S026\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Have you noticed that you don't enjoy things as much as you used to, or that you've lost interest in activities you once found pleasurable?\n\nPatient: Oh, yes. Yes, I have. It's actually funny you ask that, because my husband was just saying the other day that my sewing room has been awfully quiet lately. I used to... well, I used to get so excited about my quilting squares. I have these beautiful floral prints I bought on sale down at the fabric store, and I had this whole pattern mapped out for a double-wedding ring quilt. I used to lay them all out on the spare bed and just feel so much joy figuring out the color blocks and the borders. It would take up my whole afternoon. But now? I walk past that room, I look at all those supplies neatly stacked in their little clear plastic bins, and I just... I feel absolutely nothing. I can't make myself care the way I used to. \n\nAnd it's not just the quilting, doctor. It's really everything. Tuesday nights have been bowling night for me and a few of the ladies from the neighborhood for, oh, going on seven years now. We even had these silly matching shirts made a few years back. I used to look forward to it all week, just the chatter and the sound of the pins and getting out of the house, but the last few months... I just can't bring myself to go. They call and call, and I just sit there and stare at the phone ringing. I don't even care about my average slipping. \n\nAnd the church, too. I've always been the one in charge of planning the potlucks for the fellowship hall. You know how it is, if you don't organize it properly and stay on top of people, you end up with six macaroni salads, a dozen store-bought dinner rolls, and not a single hot main dish. I used to love organizing those sign-up sheets, calling people up, making sure everything was perfect and welcoming for the congregation. I took pride in it. But lately, I look at the church calendar hanging on my fridge, and it's just... it's just a piece of paper. The dates are coming up and I just can't muster up an ounce of enthusiasm to pick up the phone or plan a menu. It's like all the color has just drained right out of my life, and all these things that used to fill up my days and make me happy just seem like... like mountains I can't climb. I just don't care anymore, and I don't understand where all that energy went.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling down or sad most of the time, even when there's no particular reason for it?\n\nPatient: Well, yes, I suppose I have. It's... it's like this constant weight, doctor. Every single morning, I wake up and before my feet even hit the floor, before I've even had a chance to think about what day it is or what I'm supposed to be doing, there's just this... this heavy, sad feeling sitting right on my chest. It's almost physical, the way it presses down on me. I'll shuffle out to the kitchen, and my kitchen, it's this lovely bright yellow, you see. My husband painted it for me a few years ago because he knows how much I love the morning sun coming through the window over the sink. And I'll get the coffee going, just like I always do, listening to the pot gurgle and spit, but it doesn't matter. I can be standing right there in the sunlight, holding a warm mug, and I just feel like all the warmth has gone out of the world. It's just cold and gray inside my head. \n\nAnd there's no reason for it! That's the part that really gets to me, doctor. I have a good life. A wonderful husband, a nice home, we're not struggling to pay the bills or anything like that. I shouldn't be feeling this way. But most days, it's just there, this deep, unshakeable sadness that follows me from room to room. I'll sit in my armchair in the living room, looking out the window at the neighbor's garden, and I'll just start crying. For absolutely no reason at all. The tears just come, and I can't stop them, and I sit there wiping my face with a tissue, feeling so foolish because nothing bad has happened. It's just this pervasive gloom, this feeling that everything is just... sad. And it doesn't lift. It just lingers there, day in and day out, making everything feel so incredibly difficult and pointless. I just don't know how to shake it off, doctor. It's like I'm stuck under a dark cloud that won't move, no matter how hard the wind blows.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any significant changes in your appetite or weight lately, like losing or gaining a lot without trying?\n\nPatient: Well, like I was trying to explain a little bit earlier with the toast and the sweets, my eating has just gotten completely, entirely turned around. In fact, that's really one of the main things that made me realize I needed to come in and see you, because it's been going on for months now and I just can't seem to get a handle on it. \n\nIt's just so unpredictable. There are days, sometimes three or four days in a row, where I'll get up and the thought of putting a real meal together is just... well, it's impossible. I'll pour my coffee, and I'll put a single slice of plain white bread in the toaster, and I'll just sit at the kitchen table picking at it. I'll tear off a little corner, take a sip of coffee, and before I know it, it's three o'clock in the afternoon and that same piece of toast is still sitting there on my napkin, hard as a rock. I just have absolutely no desire for it. \n\nBut then, the very next week, it will swing in the exact opposite direction. I'll be doing my regular shopping down at the grocery store, and I'll just find myself standing there in the middle of the bakery aisle. I'll be staring at all those plastic containers on the tables—the muffins, the brownies, those soft sugar cookies with the thick layer of bright pink frosting and the little round sprinkles. And I'll buy them. I'll buy two or three packages at a time, bring them home, and just eat them one after another. And the most bizarre part of it all, doctor, is that I don't even really enjoy them! They just taste like pure sugar and shortening, not like a nice homemade treat at all, but I just keep eating them anyway. \n\nSo with all that back and forth, you can imagine what a mess it is. My appetite is just all over the place, and because of it, my clothes are fitting so strangely. One week the waistband on my favorite slacks is hanging loose, and the next week it's pinching me because of all those bakery cookies. It's just exhausting, having my body's routine so completely turned upside down for months on end like this.\n\n[Last Question]\nHave you noticed that you're feeling much more tired or that you're lacking energy for even simple tasks most days?\n\nRules:\n- Return exactly ONE item. If the question touches several topics, return the one it is\n  most directly about — never more than one.\n- Copy `key`, `name` and `description` exactly as they appear in [Symptom Profiles].\n- If your profile does not cover what the doctor asked, return the text \"None\" for `key`,\n  `name` and `description`, and say why in `reason`. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Oh, heavens, yes. Yes, I am just... I am so bone-tired all the time. And it doesn't even make sense, because it's not like I'm out digging ditches or anything. It's just... well, my aunt lives with us, you see. We took her in a while back, and sometimes she needs things in the middle of the night. But even on the good nights—even after a full, completely uninterrupted night of sleep when my aunt doesn't call out for me once—I still wake up feeling just completely drained. 

I'll get up, and by the time breakfast is over, I feel completely worn down. And I'm not making a big spread, just, you know, getting something simple together, but I'll be standing there at the counter and I just feel this heavy exhaustion in my arms and my legs. It's like my battery is on empty before the day has even really started. 

And the simplest little chores... they just take so much more out of me than they ever should. The laundry, for instance. I have this white plastic laundry basket, the same one I've used for years, and just carrying it from the bedroom down the hall to the washing machine... I have to stop and catch my breath. It feels like I'm lugging a basket full of bricks. I'll just leave it sitting there in the hallway because I don't have the strength to sort the darks from the lights. 

Even just making phone calls! I know I told you earlier about avoiding the church ladies and the bowling, but even just picking up the receiver to pay a bill or, I don't know, check in on my sister... it just feels like this enormous, impossible hurdle. It takes so much energy just to hold a conversation. I just don't have the stamina for any of it anymore, doctor, and I just end up sitting in my chair feeling completely wiped out by ten in the morning. I used to be on my feet all day, and now just getting through a normal morning routine feels like I've run a marathon.

It's... it's like this constant weight, doctor. Every single morning, I wake up and before my feet even hit the floor, before I've even had a chance to think about what day it is or what I'm supposed to be doing, there's just this... this heavy, sad feeling sitting right on my chest. It's almost physical, the way it presses down on me. I'll shuffle out to the kitchen, and my kitchen, it's this lovely bright yellow, you see. My husband painted it for me a few years ago because he knows how much I love the morning sun coming through the window over the sink. And I'll get the coffee going, just like I always do, listening to the pot gurgle and spit, but it doesn't matter. I can be standing right there in the sunlight, holding a warm mug, and I just feel like all the warmth has gone out of the world. It's just cold and gray inside my head. \n\nAnd there's no reason for it! That's the part that really gets to me, doctor. I have a good life. A wonderful husband, a nice home, we're not struggling to pay the bills or anything like that. I shouldn't be feeling this way. It's just this pervasive gloom, this feeling that everything is just... sad. And it doesn't lift. I'll tear off a little corner, take a sip of coffee, and before I know it, it's three o'clock in the afternoon and that same piece of toast is still sitting there on my napkin, hard as a rock. I just have absolutely no desire for it. \n\nBut then, the very next week, it will swing in the exact opposite direction. I know I told you earlier about avoiding the church ladies and the bowling, but even just picking up the receiver to pay a bill or, I don't know, check in on my sister... it just feels like this enormous, impossible hurdle. It takes so much energy just to hold a conversation. I just don't have the stamina for any of it anymore, doctor, and I just end up sitting in my chair feeling completely wiped out by ten in the morning. I used to be on my feet all day, and now just getting through a normal morning routine feels like I've run a marathon.\n\n[Last Question]\nHave you been having trouble sleeping lately, like waking up in the middle of the night or early in the morning and not being able to go back to sleep, or on the flip side, do you find yourself sleeping much more than usual?\n\n[Patient Info]\n- demographics: Female / 50 / not_in_workforce / high_school\n- severity: Mild\n\n[Targeted Info]\nNone\n\n[Conversation Style]\nYou are a Verbose patient.\nA verbose patient may\n1) provide excessively detailed responses when describing their symptoms and experiences, even when the details are directly relevant,\n2) elaborate extensively on the background, events, thoughts, feelings, and personal experiences surrounding their symptoms, and\n3) demonstrate difficulty allowing the doctor to guide the interview or move on to another clinical topic.\nThis is how you are, not a performance you put on in every reply — let it show as much or as little as the moment calls for.\n\nWhat to do:\n- Answer the doctor's last question with what [Targeted Info] holds, in your own words.\n- You are speaking out loud, not reading a line you prepared. People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there. Do not fall back on the same phrase every time.\n\nWhat not to do:\n- Don't bring up symptoms the doctor did not ask about, even ones you have.\n- Don't state a clinical fact that is not in [Targeted Info] — no other symptoms, medications,\n  test results, dates, or events.\n- Don't confirm a specific the doctor names if [Targeted Info] does not contain it. Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says. You may minimize it or hold back, never contradict it.\n- Don't use diagnostic terms or name your disorder.\n- Don't write anything but your reply, in English — no labels, quotation marks, or narration.\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Reply to the doctor's last question now, as the patient."
  }
]
========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Well, it's funny you should ask about sleep, doctor, because it's... well, it's not really an issue for me, not in the way you're describing. I mean, my sleep itself is mostly fine. I'm not tossing and turning all night, and I'm certainly not waking up at three in the morning staring at the ceiling, unable to drift back off. I know some people get like that, my neighbor Mrs. Higgins down the street, she tells me she's up half the night pacing the floor, but that's not me at all. 

When I go to bed, I sleep. And I'm not sleeping the day away either, you know? I don't stay in bed until noon or take long naps in the afternoon. I still get up at my regular time, right around six-thirty, just like I have for the last twenty years. It's just that, like I was saying before, even when I do sleep right through the night—even when my aunt is quiet and I get a solid eight hours—I still wake up feeling just completely exhausted. So the sleep itself seems normal, I'm just not getting any real rest from it, if that makes sense. But as far as actually falling asleep or staying asleep, or sleeping too much, no, I haven't noticed any changes there.

From the dialogue so far and your candidates from the previous turn, list the disorders this patient could have.\n\n[Current Turn]\n7\n\n[Previous Candidates]\nF32.1, F43.22\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hello, welcome to the clinic. Can you tell me what brings you in today?\n\nPatient: Well, doctor, I... I suppose I came in because things have just been so off lately, especially with my eating. It's been going on for months now, this whole ordeal with food. It's like my stomach and my brain aren't even talking to each other anymore. Some days, I wake up and the very thought of food is just... it's too much. I'll spend the entire day just picking at a piece of dry toast, maybe nursing a cup of black coffee until it goes cold, and that's it. That's all I can manage. But then, out of nowhere, it swings the other way. I'll find myself wandering the aisles of the grocery store, and I'll just stand there in the bakery section, staring at all the cakes and cookies. And I buy them. I buy so many sweets, things I know I shouldn't even be eating, things I don't even really enjoy that much. I'll sit at my kitchen table and just eat them, one after another, and I don't even taste them half the time. It's just this... this compulsion, I guess you could call it. And it's so frustrating because I've always been fairly regular with my meals, you know? Breakfast, lunch, dinner, maybe a little snack in the afternoon. But now, it's just this chaotic mess, and I don't know how to fix it. It's like I have no control over my own body anymore.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself overly concerned about your weight or body shape, and have you been intentionally restricting the amount of food you eat on a regular basis?\n\nPatient: No, no, it's not like that at all. I mean, I'm not sitting here counting calories or obsessing over the scale, you know? I've never been one of those women who stands in front of the mirror pinching every little bit of flesh, worrying about fitting into a certain dress size. I'm 50 years old, for heaven's sake, I've lived in this body long enough to just let it be. And I'm certainly not starving myself on purpose. When I don't eat, when I just sit there with that dry toast I mentioned, it's not because I'm trying to lose weight or because I think I'm too heavy. It's just... the desire is gone. The physical sensation of hunger just completely vanishes, like someone flipped a switch in my brain and turned off the part that tells me I need sustenance. And then, when I do eat all those sweets, it's not a failure of some diet I'm on. It's just this strange, hollow feeling I'm trying to fill, even though I know a dozen cookies won't actually do it. So, no, I wouldn't say I'm concerned about my shape, not in the way you're asking. It's more about how disconnected I feel from the whole process of nourishing myself. It's just so bizarre, doctor, to have your body do these things without any real reason or plan behind it.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed that you don't enjoy things as much as you used to, or that you've lost interest in activities you once found pleasurable?\n\nPatient: Oh, yes. Yes, I have. It's actually funny you ask that, because my husband was just saying the other day that my sewing room has been awfully quiet lately. I used to... well, I used to get so excited about my quilting squares. I have these beautiful floral prints I bought on sale down at the fabric store, and I had this whole pattern mapped out for a double-wedding ring quilt. I used to lay them all out on the spare bed and just feel so much joy figuring out the color blocks and the borders. It would take up my whole afternoon. But now? I walk past that room, I look at all those supplies neatly stacked in their little clear plastic bins, and I just... I feel absolutely nothing. I can't make myself care the way I used to. \n\nAnd it's not just the quilting, doctor. It's really everything. Tuesday nights have been bowling night for me and a few of the ladies from the neighborhood for, oh, going on seven years now. We even had these silly matching shirts made a few years back. I used to look forward to it all week, just the chatter and the sound of the pins and getting out of the house, but the last few months... I just can't bring myself to go. They call and call, and I just sit there and stare at the phone ringing. I don't even care about my average slipping. \n\nAnd the church, too. I've always been the one in charge of planning the potlucks for the fellowship hall. You know how it is, if you don't organize it properly and stay on top of people, you end up with six macaroni salads, a dozen store-bought dinner rolls, and not a single hot main dish. I used to love organizing those sign-up sheets, calling people up, making sure everything was perfect and welcoming for the congregation. I took pride in it. But lately, I look at the church calendar hanging on my fridge, and it's just... it's just a piece of paper. The dates are coming up and I just can't muster up an ounce of enthusiasm to pick up the phone or plan a menu. It's like all the color has just drained right out of my life, and all these things that used to fill up my days and make me happy just seem like... like mountains I can't climb. I just don't care anymore, and I don't understand where all that energy went.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling down or sad most of the time, even when there's no particular reason for it?\n\nPatient: Well, yes, I suppose I have. It's... it's like this constant weight, doctor. Every single morning, I wake up and before my feet even hit the floor, before I've even had a chance to think about what day it is or what I'm supposed to be doing, there's just this... this heavy, sad feeling sitting right on my chest. It's almost physical, the way it presses down on me. I'll shuffle out to the kitchen, and my kitchen, it's this lovely bright yellow, you see. My husband painted it for me a few years ago because he knows how much I love the morning sun coming through the window over the sink. And I'll get the coffee going, just like I always do, listening to the pot gurgle and spit, but it doesn't matter. I can be standing right there in the sunlight, holding a warm mug, and I just feel like all the warmth has gone out of the world. It's just cold and gray inside my head. \n\nAnd there's no reason for it! That's the part that really gets to me, doctor. I have a good life. A wonderful husband, a nice home, we're not struggling to pay the bills or anything like that. I shouldn't be feeling this way. But most days, it's just there, this deep, unshakeable sadness that follows me from room to room. I'll sit in my armchair in the living room, looking out the window at the neighbor's garden, and I'll just start crying. For absolutely no reason at all. The tears just come, and I can't stop them, and I sit there wiping my face with a tissue, feeling so foolish because nothing bad has happened. It's just this pervasive gloom, this feeling that everything is just... sad. And it doesn't lift. It just lingers there, day in and day out, making everything feel so incredibly difficult and pointless. I just don't know how to shake it off, doctor. It's like I'm stuck under a dark cloud that won't move, no matter how hard the wind blows.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any significant changes in your appetite or weight lately, like losing or gaining a lot without trying?\n\nPatient: Well, like I was trying to explain a little bit earlier with the toast and the sweets, my eating has just gotten completely, entirely turned around. In fact, that's really one of the main things that made me realize I needed to come in and see you, because it's been going on for months now and I just can't seem to get a handle on it. \n\nIt's just so unpredictable. There are days, sometimes three or four days in a row, where I'll get up and the thought of putting a real meal together is just... well, it's impossible. I'll pour my coffee, and I'll put a single slice of plain white bread in the toaster, and I'll just sit at the kitchen table picking at it. I'll tear off a little corner, take a sip of coffee, and before I know it, it's three o'clock in the afternoon and that same piece of toast is still sitting there on my napkin, hard as a rock. I just have absolutely no desire for it. \n\nBut then, the very next week, it will swing in the exact opposite direction. I'll be doing my regular shopping down at the grocery store, and I'll just find myself standing there in the middle of the bakery aisle. I'll be staring at all those plastic containers on the tables—the muffins, the brownies, those soft sugar cookies with the thick layer of bright pink frosting and the little round sprinkles. And I'll buy them. I'll buy two or three packages at a time, bring them home, and just eat them one after another. And the most bizarre part of it all, doctor, is that I don't even really enjoy them! They just taste like pure sugar and shortening, not like a nice homemade treat at all, but I just keep eating them anyway. \n\nSo with all that back and forth, you can imagine what a mess it is. My appetite is just all over the place, and because of it, my clothes are fitting so strangely. One week the waistband on my favorite slacks is hanging loose, and the next week it's pinching me because of all those bakery cookies. It's just exhausting, having my body's routine so completely turned upside down for months on end like this.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed that you're feeling much more tired or that you're lacking energy for even simple tasks most days?\n\nPatient: Oh, heavens, yes. Yes, I am just... I am so bone-tired all the time. And it doesn't even make sense, because it's not like I'm out digging ditches or anything. It's just... well, my aunt lives with us, you see. We took her in a while back, and sometimes she needs things in the middle of the night. But even on the good nights—even after a full, completely uninterrupted night of sleep when my aunt doesn't call out for me once—I still wake up feeling just completely drained. \n\nI'll get up, and by the time breakfast is over, I feel completely worn down. And I'm not making a big spread, just, you know, getting something simple together, but I'll be standing there at the counter and I just feel this heavy exhaustion in my arms and my legs. It's like my battery is on empty before the day has even really started. \n\nAnd the simplest little chores... they just take so much more out of me than they ever should. The laundry, for instance. I have this white plastic laundry basket, the same one I've used for years, and just carrying it from the bedroom down the hall to the washing machine... I have to stop and catch my breath. It feels like I'm lugging a basket full of bricks. I'll just leave it sitting there in the hallway because I don't have the strength to sort the darks from the lights. \n\nEven just making phone calls! I know I told you earlier about avoiding the church ladies and the bowling, but even just picking up the receiver to pay a bill or, I don't know, check in on my sister... it just feels like this enormous, impossible hurdle. It takes so much energy just to hold a conversation. I just don't have the stamina for any of it anymore, doctor, and I just end up sitting in my chair feeling completely wiped out by ten in the morning. I used to be on my feet all day, and now just getting through a normal morning routine feels like I've run a marathon.\n\nDoctor: Have you been having trouble sleeping lately, like waking up in the middle of the night or early in the morning and not being able to go back to sleep, or on the flip side, do you find yourself sleeping much more than usual?\n\nPatient: Well, it's funny you should ask about sleep, doctor, because it's... well, it's not really an issue for me, not in the way you're describing. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Well, it's definitely the first one. The slowing down. I'm certainly not bouncing off the walls or fidgeting, I just don't have the energy for that, like I was telling you before. But as for moving sluggishly... yes. It's actually gotten to the point where other people are pointing it out, which is just so embarrassing for me. 

Just the other day, I was at the grocery store—the Kroger over on Fifth Street, you know the one with the big floral department right when you walk in—and I was just trying to get my shopping done. And I felt like I was moving through thick mud. Every single step just took so much deliberate effort. And an old acquaintance of mine came up behind me in the produce section, and she actually stopped and asked me if my hip was bothering me! She said I was moving so slow and shuffling along so much that she thought I might be needing a hip replacement soon. And I didn't even know what to say to her, because my hips are perfectly fine! I've never had joint problems in my life, knock on wood, but I just couldn't seem to make my legs move any faster than a slow drag past the apples. 

And it's not just the walking, either. It's... well, it's like my brain and my mouth are moving through that same mud. I still go to these little community board meetings once a month—well, I try to, anyway, when I can muster the strength to get out the door—and someone will ask me the absolute simplest thing. They'll say, "Did you remember to bring the sign-in sheet?" or "What do you think about the new parking rules?" And I just sit there. I hear them perfectly fine, but it takes me just absolutely forever to gather my thoughts and actually push the words out of my mouth to answer them. It's like there's this long, terrible pause where everyone is just staring at me across the folding table, waiting, and I'm just trying to find the spark to form a basic sentence. So, yes, it's a very noticeable sluggishness, both in how I walk and how I talk, and people are definitely starting to ask questions about it because I'm just not my usual quick self at all.

Can you tell me what brings you in today?\n\nPatient: Well, doctor, I... I suppose I came in because things have just been so off lately, especially with my eating. It's been going on for months now, this whole ordeal with food. It's like my stomach and my brain aren't even talking to each other anymore. Some days, I wake up and the very thought of food is just... it's too much. I'll spend the entire day just picking at a piece of dry toast, maybe nursing a cup of black coffee until it goes cold, and that's it. That's all I can manage. But then, out of nowhere, it swings the other way. I'll find myself wandering the aisles of the grocery store, and I'll just stand there in the bakery section, staring at all the cakes and cookies. And I buy them. I buy so many sweets, things I know I shouldn't even be eating, things I don't even really enjoy that much. I'll sit at my kitchen table and just eat them, one after another, and I don't even taste them half the time. It's just this... this compulsion, I guess you could call it. And it's so frustrating because I've always been fairly regular with my meals, you know? Breakfast, lunch, dinner, maybe a little snack in the afternoon. But now, it's just this chaotic mess, and I don't know how to fix it. It's like I have no control over my own body anymore.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself overly concerned about your weight or body shape, and have you been intentionally restricting the amount of food you eat on a regular basis?\n\nPatient: No, no, it's not like that at all. I mean, I'm not sitting here counting calories or obsessing over the scale, you know? I've never been one of those women who stands in front of the mirror pinching every little bit of flesh, worrying about fitting into a certain dress size. I'm 50 years old, for heaven's sake, I've lived in this body long enough to just let it be. And I'm certainly not starving myself on purpose. When I don't eat, when I just sit there with that dry toast I mentioned, it's not because I'm trying to lose weight or because I think I'm too heavy. It's just... the desire is gone. The physical sensation of hunger just completely vanishes, like someone flipped a switch in my brain and turned off the part that tells me I need sustenance. And then, when I do eat all those sweets, it's not a failure of some diet I'm on. It's just this strange, hollow feeling I'm trying to fill, even though I know a dozen cookies won't actually do it. So, no, I wouldn't say I'm concerned about my shape, not in the way you're asking. It's more about how disconnected I feel from the whole process of nourishing myself. It's just so bizarre, doctor, to have your body do these things without any real reason or plan behind it.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed that you don't enjoy things as much as you used to, or that you've lost interest in activities you once found pleasurable?\n\nPatient: Oh, yes. Yes, I have. It's actually funny you ask that, because my husband was just saying the other day that my sewing room has been awfully quiet lately. I used to... well, I used to get so excited about my quilting squares. I have these beautiful floral prints I bought on sale down at the fabric store, and I had this whole pattern mapped out for a double-wedding ring quilt. I used to lay them all out on the spare bed and just feel so much joy figuring out the color blocks and the borders. It would take up my whole afternoon. But now? I walk past that room, I look at all those supplies neatly stacked in their little clear plastic bins, and I just... I feel absolutely nothing. I can't make myself care the way I used to. \n\nAnd it's not just the quilting, doctor. It's really everything. Tuesday nights have been bowling night for me and a few of the ladies from the neighborhood for, oh, going on seven years now. We even had these silly matching shirts made a few years back. I used to look forward to it all week, just the chatter and the sound of the pins and getting out of the house, but the last few months... I just can't bring myself to go. They call and call, and I just sit there and stare at the phone ringing. I don't even care about my average slipping. \n\nAnd the church, too. I've always been the one in charge of planning the potlucks for the fellowship hall. You know how it is, if you don't organize it properly and stay on top of people, you end up with six macaroni salads, a dozen store-bought dinner rolls, and not a single hot main dish. I used to love organizing those sign-up sheets, calling people up, making sure everything was perfect and welcoming for the congregation. I took pride in it. But lately, I look at the church calendar hanging on my fridge, and it's just... it's just a piece of paper. The dates are coming up and I just can't muster up an ounce of enthusiasm to pick up the phone or plan a menu. It's like all the color has just drained right out of my life, and all these things that used to fill up my days and make me happy just seem like... like mountains I can't climb. I just don't care anymore, and I don't understand where all that energy went.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling down or sad most of the time, even when there's no particular reason for it?\n\nPatient: Well, yes, I suppose I have. It's... it's like this constant weight, doctor. Every single morning, I wake up and before my feet even hit the floor, before I've even had a chance to think about what day it is or what I'm supposed to be doing, there's just this... this heavy, sad feeling sitting right on my chest. It's almost physical, the way it presses down on me. I'll shuffle out to the kitchen, and my kitchen, it's this lovely bright yellow, you see. My husband painted it for me a few years ago because he knows how much I love the morning sun coming through the window over the sink. And I'll get the coffee going, just like I always do, listening to the pot gurgle and spit, but it doesn't matter. I can be standing right there in the sunlight, holding a warm mug, and I just feel like all the warmth has gone out of the world. It's just cold and gray inside my head. \n\nAnd there's no reason for it! That's the part that really gets to me, doctor. I have a good life. A wonderful husband, a nice home, we're not struggling to pay the bills or anything like that. I shouldn't be feeling this way. But most days, it's just there, this deep, unshakeable sadness that follows me from room to room. I'll sit in my armchair in the living room, looking out the window at the neighbor's garden, and I'll just start crying. For absolutely no reason at all. The tears just come, and I can't stop them, and I sit there wiping my face with a tissue, feeling so foolish because nothing bad has happened. It's just this pervasive gloom, this feeling that everything is just... sad. And it doesn't lift. It just lingers there, day in and day out, making everything feel so incredibly difficult and pointless. I just don't know how to shake it off, doctor. I'll tear off a little corner, take a sip of coffee, and before I know it, it's three o'clock in the afternoon and that same piece of toast is still sitting there on my napkin, hard as a rock. I just have absolutely no desire for it. \n\nBut then, the very next week, it will swing in the exact opposite direction. I'll be doing my regular shopping down at the grocery store, and I'll just find myself standing there in the middle of the bakery aisle. I'll be staring at all those plastic containers on the tables—the muffins, the brownies, those soft sugar cookies with the thick layer of bright pink frosting and the little round sprinkles. And I'll buy them. I'll buy two or three packages at a time, bring them home, and just eat them one after another. And the most bizarre part of it all, doctor, is that I don't even really enjoy them! They just taste like pure sugar and shortening, not like a nice homemade treat at all, but I just keep eating them anyway. \n\nSo with all that back and forth, you can imagine what a mess it is. My appetite is just all over the place, and because of it, my clothes are fitting so strangely. One week the waistband on my favorite slacks is hanging loose, and the next week it's pinching me because of all those bakery cookies. It's just exhausting, having my body's routine so completely turned upside down for months on end like this.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed that you're feeling much more tired or that you're lacking energy for even simple tasks most days?\n\nPatient: Oh, heavens, yes. Yes, I am just... I am so bone-tired all the time. And it doesn't even make sense, because it's not like I'm out digging ditches or anything. It's just... well, my aunt lives with us, you see. We took her in a while back, and sometimes she needs things in the middle of the night. But even on the good nights—even after a full, completely uninterrupted night of sleep when my aunt doesn't call out for me once—I still wake up feeling just completely drained. \n\nI'll get up, and by the time breakfast is over, I feel completely worn down. And I'm not making a big spread, just, you know, getting something simple together, but I'll be standing there at the counter and I just feel this heavy exhaustion in my arms and my legs. It's like my battery is on empty before the day has even really started. \n\nAnd the simplest little chores... they just take so much more out of me than they ever should. The laundry, for instance. I have this white plastic laundry basket, the same one I've used for years, and just carrying it from the bedroom down the hall to the washing machine... I have to stop and catch my breath. It feels like I'm lugging a basket full of bricks. I'll just leave it sitting there in the hallway because I don't have the strength to sort the darks from the lights. \n\nEven just making phone calls! I know I told you earlier about avoiding the church ladies and the bowling, but even just picking up the receiver to pay a bill or, I don't know, check in on my sister... it just feels like this enormous, impossible hurdle. It takes so much energy just to hold a conversation. I just don't have the stamina for any of it anymore, doctor, and I just end up sitting in my chair feeling completely wiped out by ten in the morning. I used to be on my feet all day, and now just getting through a normal morning routine feels like I've run a marathon.\n\nDoctor: Have you been having trouble sleeping lately, like waking up in the middle of the night or early in the morning and not being able to go back to sleep, or on the flip side, do you find yourself sleeping much more than usual?\n\nPatient: Well, it's funny you should ask about sleep, doctor, because it's... well, it's not really an issue for me, not in the way you're describing. I mean, my sleep itself is mostly fine. I'm not tossing and turning all night, and I'm certainly not waking up at three in the morning staring at the ceiling, unable to drift back off. I know some people get like that, my neighbor Mrs. Higgins down the street, she tells me she's up half the night pacing the floor, but that's not me at all. \n\nWhen I go to bed, I sleep. And I'm not sleeping the day away either, you know? I don't stay in bed until noon or take long naps in the afternoon. I still get up at my regular time, right around six-thirty, just like I have for the last twenty years. It's just that, like I was saying before, even when I do sleep right through the night—even when my aunt is quiet and I get a solid eight hours—I still wake up feeling just completely exhausted. So the sleep itself seems normal, I'm just not getting any real rest from it, if that makes sense. But as far as actually falling asleep or staying asleep, or sleeping too much, no, I haven't noticed any changes there.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed that your movements have slowed down or that you're moving more sluggishly than usual, or on the flip side, that you're more restless or fidgety without being able to sit still?\n\nPatient: Well, it's definitely the first one. The slowing down. I'm certainly not bouncing off the walls or fidgeting, I just don't have the energy for that, like I was telling you before. But as for moving sluggishly... yes. It's actually gotten to the point where other people are pointing it out, which is just so embarrassing for me. \n\nJust the other day, I was at the grocery store—the Kroger over on Fifth Street, you know the one with the big floral department right when you walk in—and I was just trying to get my shopping done. And I felt like I was moving through thick mud. Every single step just took so much deliberate effort. And an old acquaintance of mine came up behind me in the produce section, and she actually stopped and asked me if my hip was bothering me! She said I was moving so slow and shuffling along so much that she thought I might be needing a hip replacement soon. And I didn't even know what to say to her, because my hips are perfectly fine! I've never had joint problems in my life, knock on wood, but I just couldn't seem to make my legs move any faster than a slow drag past the apples. \n\nAnd it's not just the walking, either. It's... well, it's like my brain and my mouth are moving through that same mud. I still go to these little community board meetings once a month—well, I try to, anyway, when I can muster the strength to get out the door—and someone will ask me the absolute simplest thing. They'll say, \"Did you remember to bring the sign-in sheet?\" or \"What do you think about the new parking rules?\" And I just sit there. I hear them perfectly fine, but it takes me just absolutely forever to gather my thoughts and actually push the words out of my mouth to answer them. It's like there's this long, terrible pause where everyone is just staring at me across the folding table, waiting, and I'm just trying to find the spark to form a basic sentence. So, yes, it's a very noticeable sluggishness, both in how I walk and how I talk, and people are definitely starting to ask questions about it because I'm just not my usual quick self at all.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. Oh, goodness, no. No, nothing like that at all. I mean, the idea of eating a lot of food right now, especially when I'm not hungry... it just sounds completely exhausting. I just don't have the appetite for it, and I definitely don't have those moments where I feel out of control with eating. Some days, I wake up and the very thought of food is just... it's too much. I'll spend the entire day just picking at a piece of dry toast, maybe nursing a cup of black coffee until it goes cold, and that's it. That's all I can manage. But then, out of nowhere, it swings the other way. I'll find myself wandering the aisles of the grocery store, and I'll just stand there in the bakery section, staring at all the cakes and cookies. I've never been one of those women who stands in front of the mirror pinching every little bit of flesh, worrying about fitting into a certain dress size. I'm 50 years old, for heaven's sake, I've lived in this body long enough to just let it be. And I'm certainly not starving myself on purpose. When I don't eat, when I just sit there with that dry toast I mentioned, it's not because I'm trying to lose weight or because I think I'm too heavy. It's just... the desire is gone. The physical sensation of hunger just completely vanishes, like someone flipped a switch in my brain and turned off the part that tells me I need sustenance. It's just so bizarre, doctor, to have your body do these things without any real reason or plan behind it.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed that you don't enjoy things as much as you used to, or that you've lost interest in activities you once found pleasurable?\n\nPatient: Oh, yes. I used to lay them all out on the spare bed and just feel so much joy figuring out the color blocks and the borders. It would take up my whole afternoon. But now? I walk past that room, I look at all those supplies neatly stacked in their little clear plastic bins, and I just... I feel absolutely nothing. I can't make myself care the way I used to. \n\nAnd it's not just the quilting, doctor. It's really everything. Tuesday nights have been bowling night for me and a few of the ladies from the neighborhood for, oh, going on seven years now. We even had these silly matching shirts made a few years back. I used to look forward to it all week, just the chatter and the sound of the pins and getting out of the house, but the last few months... I just can't bring myself to go. They call and call, and I just sit there and stare at the phone ringing. I don't even care about my average slipping. \n\nAnd the church, too. I've always been the one in charge of planning the potlucks for the fellowship hall. You know how it is, if you don't organize it properly and stay on top of people, you end up with six macaroni salads, a dozen store-bought dinner rolls, and not a single hot main dish. I used to love organizing those sign-up sheets, calling people up, making sure everything was perfect and welcoming for the congregation. I took pride in it. But lately, I look at the church calendar hanging on my fridge, and it's just... it's just a piece of paper. The dates are coming up and I just can't muster up an ounce of enthusiasm to pick up the phone or plan a menu. It's like all the color has just drained right out of my life, and all these things that used to fill up my days and make me happy just seem like... like mountains I can't climb. I just don't care anymore, and I don't understand where all that energy went.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling down or sad most of the time, even when there's no particular reason for it?\n\nPatient: Well, yes, I suppose I have. It's... it's like this constant weight, doctor. Every single morning, I wake up and before my feet even hit the floor, before I've even had a chance to think about what day it is or what I'm supposed to be doing, there's just this... this heavy, sad feeling sitting right on my chest. It's almost physical, the way it presses down on me. I'll shuffle out to the kitchen, and my kitchen, it's this lovely bright yellow, you see. My husband painted it for me a few years ago because he knows how much I love the morning sun coming through the window over the sink. And I'll get the coffee going, just like I always do, listening to the pot gurgle and spit, but it doesn't matter. I can be standing right there in the sunlight, holding a warm mug, and I just feel like all the warmth has gone out of the world. It's just cold and gray inside my head. \n\nAnd there's no reason for it! That's the part that really gets to me, doctor. I have a good life. A wonderful husband, a nice home, we're not struggling to pay the bills or anything like that. I shouldn't be feeling this way. But most days, it's just there, this deep, unshakeable sadness that follows me from room to room. I'll sit in my armchair in the living room, looking out the window at the neighbor's garden, and I'll just start crying. For absolutely no reason at all. The tears just come, and I can't stop them, and I sit there wiping my face with a tissue, feeling so foolish because nothing bad has happened. It's just this pervasive gloom, this feeling that everything is just... sad. And it doesn't lift. It just lingers there, day in and day out, making everything feel so incredibly difficult and pointless. I just don't know how to shake it off, doctor. It's like I'm stuck under a dark cloud that won't move, no matter how hard the wind blows.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any significant changes in your appetite or weight lately, like losing or gaining a lot without trying?\n\nPatient: Well, like I was trying to explain a little bit earlier with the toast and the sweets, my eating has just gotten completely, entirely turned around. I'll pour my coffee, and I'll put a single slice of plain white bread in the toaster, and I'll just sit at the kitchen table picking at it. I'll tear off a little corner, take a sip of coffee, and before I know it, it's three o'clock in the afternoon and that same piece of toast is still sitting there on my napkin, hard as a rock. I just have absolutely no desire for it. \n\nBut then, the very next week, it will swing in the exact opposite direction. I'll be doing my regular shopping down at the grocery store, and I'll just find myself standing there in the middle of the bakery aisle. I'll be staring at all those plastic containers on the tables—the muffins, the brownies, those soft sugar cookies with the thick layer of bright pink frosting and the little round sprinkles. And I'll buy them. I'll buy two or three packages at a time, bring them home, and just eat them one after another. And the most bizarre part of it all, doctor, is that I don't even really enjoy them! They just taste like pure sugar and shortening, not like a nice homemade treat at all, but I just keep eating them anyway. \n\nSo with all that back and forth, you can imagine what a mess it is. My appetite is just all over the place, and because of it, my clothes are fitting so strangely. One week the waistband on my favorite slacks is hanging loose, and the next week it's pinching me because of all those bakery cookies. It's just exhausting, having my body's routine so completely turned upside down for months on end like this.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed that you're feeling much more tired or that you're lacking energy for even simple tasks most days?\n\nPatient: Oh, heavens, yes. Yes, I am just... I am so bone-tired all the time. And it doesn't even make sense, because it's not like I'm out digging ditches or anything. It's just... well, my aunt lives with us, you see. We took her in a while back, and sometimes she needs things in the middle of the night. But even on the good nights—even after a full, completely uninterrupted night of sleep when my aunt doesn't call out for me once—I still wake up feeling just completely drained. \n\nI'll get up, and by the time breakfast is over, I feel completely worn down. And I'm not making a big spread, just, you know, getting something simple together, but I'll be standing there at the counter and I just feel this heavy exhaustion in my arms and my legs. It's like my battery is on empty before the day has even really started. \n\nAnd the simplest little chores... they just take so much more out of me than they ever should. The laundry, for instance. I have this white plastic laundry basket, the same one I've used for years, and just carrying it from the bedroom down the hall to the washing machine... I have to stop and catch my breath. It feels like I'm lugging a basket full of bricks. I'll just leave it sitting there in the hallway because I don't have the strength to sort the darks from the lights. \n\nEven just making phone calls! I know I told you earlier about avoiding the church ladies and the bowling, but even just picking up the receiver to pay a bill or, I don't know, check in on my sister... it just feels like this enormous, impossible hurdle. It takes so much energy just to hold a conversation. I just don't have the stamina for any of it anymore, doctor, and I just end up sitting in my chair feeling completely wiped out by ten in the morning. I used to be on my feet all day, and now just getting through a normal morning routine feels like I've run a marathon.\n\nDoctor: Have you been having trouble sleeping lately, like waking up in the middle of the night or early in the morning and not being able to go back to sleep, or on the flip side, do you find yourself sleeping much more than usual?\n\nPatient: Well, it's funny you should ask about sleep, doctor, because it's... well, it's not really an issue for me, not in the way you're describing. I mean, my sleep itself is mostly fine. I'm not tossing and turning all night, and I'm certainly not waking up at three in the morning staring at the ceiling, unable to drift back off. I know some people get like that, my neighbor Mrs. Higgins down the street, she tells me she's up half the night pacing the floor, but that's not me at all. \n\nWhen I go to bed, I sleep. And I'm not sleeping the day away either, you know? I don't stay in bed until noon or take long naps in the afternoon. I still get up at my regular time, right around six-thirty, just like I have for the last twenty years. It's just that, like I was saying before, even when I do sleep right through the night—even when my aunt is quiet and I get a solid eight hours—I still wake up feeling just completely exhausted. So the sleep itself seems normal, I'm just not getting any real rest from it, if that makes sense. But as far as actually falling asleep or staying asleep, or sleeping too much, no, I haven't noticed any changes there.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed that your movements have slowed down or that you're moving more sluggishly than usual, or on the flip side, that you're more restless or fidgety without being able to sit still?\n\nPatient: Well, it's definitely the first one. The slowing down. I'm certainly not bouncing off the walls or fidgeting, I just don't have the energy for that, like I was telling you before. But as for moving sluggishly... yes. It's actually gotten to the point where other people are pointing it out, which is just so embarrassing for me. \n\nJust the other day, I was at the grocery store—the Kroger over on Fifth Street, you know the one with the big floral department right when you walk in—and I was just trying to get my shopping done. And I felt like I was moving through thick mud. Every single step just took so much deliberate effort. And an old acquaintance of mine came up behind me in the produce section, and she actually stopped and asked me if my hip was bothering me! She said I was moving so slow and shuffling along so much that she thought I might be needing a hip replacement soon. And I didn't even know what to say to her, because my hips are perfectly fine! I've never had joint problems in my life, knock on wood, but I just couldn't seem to make my legs move any faster than a slow drag past the apples. \n\nAnd it's not just the walking, either. It's... well, it's like my brain and my mouth are moving through that same mud. I still go to these little community board meetings once a month—well, I try to, anyway, when I can muster the strength to get out the door—and someone will ask me the absolute simplest thing. They'll say, \"Did you remember to bring the sign-in sheet?\" or \"What do you think about the new parking rules?\" And I just sit there. I hear them perfectly fine, but it takes me just absolutely forever to gather my thoughts and actually push the words out of my mouth to answer them. It's like there's this long, terrible pause where everyone is just staring at me across the folding table, waiting, and I'm just trying to find the spark to form a basic sentence. So, yes, it's a very noticeable sluggishness, both in how I walk and how I talk, and people are definitely starting to ask questions about it because I'm just not my usual quick self at all.\n\nDoctor: Have you had repeated episodes of eating a lot of food in a short amount of time, even when you're not hungry, and felt like you couldn't stop yourself once you started?\n\nPatient: Oh, goodness, no. No, nothing like that at all. I mean, the idea of eating a lot of food right now, especially when I'm not hungry... it just sounds completely exhausting. Like I was saying before about having no energy, even just making a simple meal feels like climbing a mountain. So sitting down and eating a huge amount of food? I can't even imagine it. \n\nI know some people eat when they're upset or stressed. My sister-in-law, Martha, she's like that. Whenever she and my brother have a spat, she'll bake an entire batch of chocolate chip cookies and eat half of them before they've even cooled on the rack. She says it comforts her, but I just don't have that kind of relationship with food. \n\nHonestly, lately, I just kind of pick at things. I'll have a piece of toast in the morning, maybe a little soup for lunch, but I'm certainly not raiding the pantry or eating until I'm stuffed. I just don't have the appetite for it, and I definitely don't have those moments where I feel out of control with eating. It's more like I have to remind myself to eat just to keep my strength up, what little strength I have left, anyway. So, no, I haven't been doing anything like that at all.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing.
