No, nothing like that at all. I mean, the idea of eating a lot of food right now, especially when I'm not hungry... it just sounds completely exhausting. Like I was saying before about having no energy, even just making a simple meal feels like climbing a mountain. So sitting down and eating a huge amount of food? I can't even imagine it. \n\nI know some people eat when they're upset or stressed. My sister-in-law, Martha, she's like that. Whenever she and my brother have a spat, she'll bake an entire batch of chocolate chip cookies and eat half of them before they've even cooled on the rack. She says it comforts her, but I just don't have that kind of relationship with food. \n\nHonestly, lately, I just kind of pick at things. I'll have a piece of toast in the morning, maybe a little soup for lunch, but I'm certainly not raiding the pantry or eating until I'm stuffed. I just don't have the appetite for it, and I definitely don't have those moments where I feel out of control with eating. It's more like I have to remind myself to eat just to keep my strength up, what little strength I have left, anyway. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Oh, no. No, I don't think like that at all. I know I've been saying how exhausting everything is, and how just getting through the day feels like such a monumental chore, but I certainly don't want to stop living. I mean, my goodness, I still have things I need to do. I have my little garden out back—well, it's mostly just hostas and a few sad-looking tomato plants right now because I haven't had the energy to weed it properly, but I still like knowing it's there. And who would take care of my cat, Barnaby? He's such a fussy thing, he only eats the chicken pate, never the beef, and he gets so anxious if I'm not up by a certain time to open his tin. 

I remember years ago, my cousin Brenda went through a very dark time after the local plant closed down, and she used to say things exactly like that, about just wanting to go to sleep and never wake up. It used to just break my heart and scare me half to death to hear her talk that way. I'd sit with her on her porch for hours, sometimes until it got pitch black out, just trying to convince her that things would turn around and that she had so much left to give. So I know what that looks like, and I know how serious it is when someone starts talking like that, but that's just not where my head is at. 

Even with the sluggishness, and the absolute drain I feel every morning, I still want to be here. I just want to feel *better* while I'm here, you know? I want to wake up and actually feel refreshed and ready to put on a pot of coffee, instead of dreading how heavy my legs are going to feel when I swing them over the edge of the mattress. But as far as actually wishing I wouldn't wake up, or thinking life isn't worth it? No, doctor, I've never had a thought like that. I just want to get back to being my normal self.

It's... it's like this constant weight, doctor. Every single morning, I wake up and before my feet even hit the floor, before I've even had a chance to think about what day it is or what I'm supposed to be doing, there's just this... this heavy, sad feeling sitting right on my chest. It's almost physical, the way it presses down on me. I'll shuffle out to the kitchen, and my kitchen, it's this lovely bright yellow, you see. My husband painted it for me a few years ago because he knows how much I love the morning sun coming through the window over the sink. And I'll get the coffee going, just like I always do, listening to the pot gurgle and spit, but it doesn't matter. I can be standing right there in the sunlight, holding a warm mug, and I just feel like all the warmth has gone out of the world. It's just cold and gray inside my head. \n\nAnd there's no reason for it! That's the part that really gets to me, doctor. I have a good life. A wonderful husband, a nice home, we're not struggling to pay the bills or anything like that. I shouldn't be feeling this way. But most days, it's just there, this deep, unshakeable sadness that follows me from room to room. I'll sit in my armchair in the living room, looking out the window at the neighbor's garden, and I'll just start crying. For absolutely no reason at all. The tears just come, and I can't stop them, and I sit there wiping my face with a tissue, feeling so foolish because nothing bad has happened. It's just this pervasive gloom, this feeling that everything is just... sad. And it doesn't lift. It just lingers there, day in and day out, making everything feel so incredibly difficult and pointless. I just don't know how to shake it off, doctor. It's like I'm stuck under a dark cloud that won't move, no matter how hard the wind blows.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any significant changes in your appetite or weight lately, like losing or gaining a lot without trying?\n\nPatient: Well, like I was trying to explain a little bit earlier with the toast and the sweets, my eating has just gotten completely, entirely turned around. In fact, that's really one of the main things that made me realize I needed to come in and see you, because it's been going on for months now and I just can't seem to get a handle on it. \n\nIt's just so unpredictable. There are days, sometimes three or four days in a row, where I'll get up and the thought of putting a real meal together is just... well, it's impossible. I'll pour my coffee, and I'll put a single slice of plain white bread in the toaster, and I'll just sit at the kitchen table picking at it. I'll tear off a little corner, take a sip of coffee, and before I know it, it's three o'clock in the afternoon and that same piece of toast is still sitting there on my napkin, hard as a rock. I just have absolutely no desire for it. \n\nBut then, the very next week, it will swing in the exact opposite direction. I'll be doing my regular shopping down at the grocery store, and I'll just find myself standing there in the middle of the bakery aisle. I'll be staring at all those plastic containers on the tables—the muffins, the brownies, those soft sugar cookies with the thick layer of bright pink frosting and the little round sprinkles. And I'll buy them. I'll buy two or three packages at a time, bring them home, and just eat them one after another. And the most bizarre part of it all, doctor, is that I don't even really enjoy them! They just taste like pure sugar and shortening, not like a nice homemade treat at all, but I just keep eating them anyway. \n\nSo with all that back and forth, you can imagine what a mess it is. My appetite is just all over the place, and because of it, my clothes are fitting so strangely. One week the waistband on my favorite slacks is hanging loose, and the next week it's pinching me because of all those bakery cookies. It's just exhausting, having my body's routine so completely turned upside down for months on end like this.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed that you're feeling much more tired or that you're lacking energy for even simple tasks most days?\n\nPatient: Oh, heavens, yes. Yes, I am just... I am so bone-tired all the time. And it doesn't even make sense, because it's not like I'm out digging ditches or anything. It's just... well, my aunt lives with us, you see. We took her in a while back, and sometimes she needs things in the middle of the night. But even on the good nights—even after a full, completely uninterrupted night of sleep when my aunt doesn't call out for me once—I still wake up feeling just completely drained. \n\nI'll get up, and by the time breakfast is over, I feel completely worn down. And I'm not making a big spread, just, you know, getting something simple together, but I'll be standing there at the counter and I just feel this heavy exhaustion in my arms and my legs. It's like my battery is on empty before the day has even really started. \n\nAnd the simplest little chores... they just take so much more out of me than they ever should. The laundry, for instance. I have this white plastic laundry basket, the same one I've used for years, and just carrying it from the bedroom down the hall to the washing machine... I have to stop and catch my breath. It feels like I'm lugging a basket full of bricks. I'll just leave it sitting there in the hallway because I don't have the strength to sort the darks from the lights. \n\nEven just making phone calls! I know I told you earlier about avoiding the church ladies and the bowling, but even just picking up the receiver to pay a bill or, I don't know, check in on my sister... it just feels like this enormous, impossible hurdle. It takes so much energy just to hold a conversation. I just don't have the stamina for any of it anymore, doctor, and I just end up sitting in my chair feeling completely wiped out by ten in the morning. I used to be on my feet all day, and now just getting through a normal morning routine feels like I've run a marathon.\n\nDoctor: Have you been having trouble sleeping lately, like waking up in the middle of the night or early in the morning and not being able to go back to sleep, or on the flip side, do you find yourself sleeping much more than usual?\n\nPatient: Well, it's funny you should ask about sleep, doctor, because it's... well, it's not really an issue for me, not in the way you're describing. I mean, my sleep itself is mostly fine. I'm not tossing and turning all night, and I'm certainly not waking up at three in the morning staring at the ceiling, unable to drift back off. I know some people get like that, my neighbor Mrs. Higgins down the street, she tells me she's up half the night pacing the floor, but that's not me at all. \n\nWhen I go to bed, I sleep. And I'm not sleeping the day away either, you know? I don't stay in bed until noon or take long naps in the afternoon. I still get up at my regular time, right around six-thirty, just like I have for the last twenty years. It's just that, like I was saying before, even when I do sleep right through the night—even when my aunt is quiet and I get a solid eight hours—I still wake up feeling just completely exhausted. So the sleep itself seems normal, I'm just not getting any real rest from it, if that makes sense. But as far as actually falling asleep or staying asleep, or sleeping too much, no, I haven't noticed any changes there.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed that your movements have slowed down or that you're moving more sluggishly than usual, or on the flip side, that you're more restless or fidgety without being able to sit still?\n\nPatient: Well, it's definitely the first one. The slowing down. I'm certainly not bouncing off the walls or fidgeting, I just don't have the energy for that, like I was telling you before. But as for moving sluggishly... yes. It's actually gotten to the point where other people are pointing it out, which is just so embarrassing for me. \n\nJust the other day, I was at the grocery store—the Kroger over on Fifth Street, you know the one with the big floral department right when you walk in—and I was just trying to get my shopping done. And I felt like I was moving through thick mud. Every single step just took so much deliberate effort. And an old acquaintance of mine came up behind me in the produce section, and she actually stopped and asked me if my hip was bothering me! She said I was moving so slow and shuffling along so much that she thought I might be needing a hip replacement soon. And I didn't even know what to say to her, because my hips are perfectly fine! I've never had joint problems in my life, knock on wood, but I just couldn't seem to make my legs move any faster than a slow drag past the apples. \n\nAnd it's not just the walking, either. It's... well, it's like my brain and my mouth are moving through that same mud. I still go to these little community board meetings once a month—well, I try to, anyway, when I can muster the strength to get out the door—and someone will ask me the absolute simplest thing. They'll say, \"Did you remember to bring the sign-in sheet?\" or \"What do you think about the new parking rules?\" And I just sit there. I hear them perfectly fine, but it takes me just absolutely forever to gather my thoughts and actually push the words out of my mouth to answer them. It's like there's this long, terrible pause where everyone is just staring at me across the folding table, waiting, and I'm just trying to find the spark to form a basic sentence. So, yes, it's a very noticeable sluggishness, both in how I walk and how I talk, and people are definitely starting to ask questions about it because I'm just not my usual quick self at all.\n\nDoctor: Have you had repeated episodes of eating a lot of food in a short amount of time, even when you're not hungry, and felt like you couldn't stop yourself once you started?\n\nPatient: Oh, goodness, no. No, nothing like that at all. I mean, the idea of eating a lot of food right now, especially when I'm not hungry... it just sounds completely exhausting. Like I was saying before about having no energy, even just making a simple meal feels like climbing a mountain. So sitting down and eating a huge amount of food? I can't even imagine it. \n\nI know some people eat when they're upset or stressed. My sister-in-law, Martha, she's like that. Whenever she and my brother have a spat, she'll bake an entire batch of chocolate chip cookies and eat half of them before they've even cooled on the rack. She says it comforts her, but I just don't have that kind of relationship with food. \n\nHonestly, lately, I just kind of pick at things. I'll have a piece of toast in the morning, maybe a little soup for lunch, but I'm certainly not raiding the pantry or eating until I'm stuffed. I just don't have the appetite for it, and I definitely don't have those moments where I feel out of control with eating. It's more like I have to remind myself to eat just to keep my strength up, what little strength I have left, anyway. So, no, I haven't been doing anything like that at all.\n\nDoctor: Have you had any thoughts about life not being worth living, or found yourself wishing you wouldn't wake up in the morning?\n\nPatient: Oh, no. No, I don't think like that at all. I know I've been saying how exhausting everything is, and how just getting through the day feels like such a monumental chore, but I certainly don't want to stop living. I mean, my goodness, I still have things I need to do. I have my little garden out back—well, it's mostly just hostas and a few sad-looking tomato plants right now because I haven't had the energy to weed it properly, but I still like knowing it's there. And who would take care of my cat, Barnaby? He's such a fussy thing, he only eats the chicken pate, never the beef, and he gets so anxious if I'm not up by a certain time to open his tin. \n\nI remember years ago, my cousin Brenda went through a very dark time after the local plant closed down, and she used to say things exactly like that, about just wanting to go to sleep and never wake up. It used to just break my heart and scare me half to death to hear her talk that way. I'd sit with her on her porch for hours, sometimes until it got pitch black out, just trying to convince her that things would turn around and that she had so much left to give. So I know what that looks like, and I know how serious it is when someone starts talking like that, but that's just not where my head is at. \n\nEven with the sluggishness, and the absolute drain I feel every morning, I still want to be here. I just want to feel *better* while I'm here, you know? I want to wake up and actually feel refreshed and ready to put on a pot of coffee, instead of dreading how heavy my legs are going to feel when I swing them over the edge of the mattress. But as far as actually wishing I wouldn't wake up, or thinking life isn't worth it? No, doctor, I've never had a thought like that. I just want to get back to being my normal self.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing.     "role": "user",
    "content": "Reply to the doctor's last question now, as the patient."

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Oh, goodness, no. Unusually energetic? That sounds like a completely foreign concept to me right now, and honestly, even when I'm feeling like my regular self, I'm just a steady, middle-of-the-road sort of person. I've never been one of those people who just buzzes around with endless energy. 

I know the type you mean, though. My neighbor, Carol—she lives two houses down, the one with the bright yellow door that she painted herself last spring—she gets like that sometimes. She'll suddenly decide she needs to reorganize her entire garage, and she'll be out there at six in the morning, hauling boxes and sweeping out dust, and she'll just keep going until the sun goes down. And she'll talk your ear off the whole time if you happen to walk by, just so full of herself and whatever new project she's dreamed up. She always says she only needs four hours of sleep when she gets into one of her "moods," as she calls them. 

But me? No, never. I've always needed my solid eight hours of sleep, sometimes nine if it's been a long week. Even before I started feeling so bogged down and heavy like I am now, I was never one to skip sleep or feel like I could conquer the world on just a few hours' rest. If I don't get a good night's sleep, I'm just useless the next day. I get a headache right behind my eyes, and I can't focus on my crossword puzzles—I usually do the one in the Sunday paper, but I haven't even been able to manage that lately. 

And as for feeling unusually confident... well, I'm just a regular person. I don't think I'm better or more special than anyone else. I've never had a time where I felt like I was on top of the world or could do no wrong. I just try to live a quiet life, take care of Barnaby, keep my house tidy—when I have the energy, anyway—and mind my own business. So, no, I've never had any periods like what you're describing. Never. I just wish I could have a fraction of normal energy back, let alone an unusual amount of it.

Every single morning, I wake up and before my feet even hit the floor, before I've even had a chance to think about what day it is or what I'm supposed to be doing, there's just this... this heavy, sad feeling sitting right on my chest. It's almost physical, the way it presses down on me. I'll shuffle out to the kitchen, and my kitchen, it's this lovely bright yellow, you see. My husband painted it for me a few years ago because he knows how much I love the morning sun coming through the window over the sink. And I'll get the coffee going, just like I always do, listening to the pot gurgle and spit, but it doesn't matter. I can be standing right there in the sunlight, holding a warm mug, and I just feel like all the warmth has gone out of the world. It's just cold and gray inside my head. \n\nAnd there's no reason for it! That's the part that really gets to me, doctor. I have a good life. It's just this pervasive gloom, this feeling that everything is just... sad. And it doesn't lift. I'll pour my coffee, and I'll put a single slice of plain white bread in the toaster, and I'll just sit at the kitchen table picking at it. I'll tear off a little corner, take a sip of coffee, and before I know it, it's three o'clock in the afternoon and that same piece of toast is still sitting there on my napkin, hard as a rock. I just have absolutely no desire for it. \n\nBut then, the very next week, it will swing in the exact opposite direction. So the sleep itself seems normal, I'm just not getting any real rest from it, if that makes sense. But as far as actually falling asleep or staying asleep, or sleeping too much, no, I haven't noticed any changes there.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed that your movements have slowed down or that you're moving more sluggishly than usual, or on the flip side, that you're more restless or fidgety without being able to sit still?\n\nPatient: Well, it's definitely the first one. The slowing down. I'm certainly not bouncing off the walls or fidgeting, I just don't have the energy for that, like I was telling you before. But as for moving sluggishly... yes. It's actually gotten to the point where other people are pointing it out, which is just so embarrassing for me. \n\nJust the other day, I was at the grocery store—the Kroger over on Fifth Street, you know the one with the big floral department right when you walk in—and I was just trying to get my shopping done. And I felt like I was moving through thick mud. Every single step just took so much deliberate effort. And an old acquaintance of mine came up behind me in the produce section, and she actually stopped and asked me if my hip was bothering me! She said I was moving so slow and shuffling along so much that she thought I might be needing a hip replacement soon. And I didn't even know what to say to her, because my hips are perfectly fine! I've never had joint problems in my life, knock on wood, but I just couldn't seem to make my legs move any faster than a slow drag past the apples. \n\nAnd it's not just the walking, either. It's... well, it's like my brain and my mouth are moving through that same mud. I still go to these little community board meetings once a month—well, I try to, anyway, when I can muster the strength to get out the door—and someone will ask me the absolute simplest thing. They'll say, \"Did you remember to bring the sign-in sheet?\" or \"What do you think about the new parking rules?\" And I just sit there. I hear them perfectly fine, but it takes me just absolutely forever to gather my thoughts and actually push the words out of my mouth to answer them. It's like there's this long, terrible pause where everyone is just staring at me across the folding table, waiting, and I'm just trying to find the spark to form a basic sentence. So, yes, it's a very noticeable sluggishness, both in how I walk and how I talk, and people are definitely starting to ask questions about it because I'm just not my usual quick self at all.\n\nDoctor: Have you had repeated episodes of eating a lot of food in a short amount of time, even when you're not hungry, and felt like you couldn't stop yourself once you started?\n\nPatient: Oh, goodness, no. No, nothing like that at all. I mean, the idea of eating a lot of food right now, especially when I'm not hungry... it just sounds completely exhausting. Like I was saying before about having no energy, even just making a simple meal feels like climbing a mountain. So sitting down and eating a huge amount of food? Whenever she and my brother have a spat, she'll bake an entire batch of chocolate chip cookies and eat half of them before they've even cooled on the rack. She says it comforts her, but I just don't have that kind of relationship with food. \n\nHonestly, lately, I just kind of pick at things. I'll have a piece of toast in the morning, maybe a little soup for lunch, but I'm certainly not raiding the pantry or eating until I'm stuffed. It's more like I have to remind myself to eat just to keep my strength up, what little strength I have left, anyway. So, no, I haven't been doing anything like that at all.\n\nDoctor: Have you had any thoughts about life not being worth living, or found yourself wishing you wouldn't wake up in the morning?\n\nPatient: Oh, no. No, I don't think like that at all. I know I've been saying how exhausting everything is, and how just getting through the day feels like such a monumental chore, but I certainly don't want to stop living. I mean, my goodness, I still have things I need to do. I have my little garden out back—well, it's mostly just hostas and a few sad-looking tomato plants right now because I haven't had the energy to weed it properly, but I still like knowing it's there. And who would take care of my cat, Barnaby? He's such a fussy thing, he only eats the chicken pate, never the beef, and he gets so anxious if I'm not up by a certain time to open his tin. \n\nI remember years ago, my cousin Brenda went through a very dark time after the local plant closed down, and she used to say things exactly like that, about just wanting to go to sleep and never wake up. It used to just break my heart and scare me half to death to hear her talk that way. I'd sit with her on her porch for hours, sometimes until it got pitch black out, just trying to convince her that things would turn around and that she had so much left to give. So I know what that looks like, and I know how serious it is when someone starts talking like that, but that's just not where my head is at. \n\nEven with the sluggishness, and the absolute drain I feel every morning, I still want to be here. I just want to feel *better* while I'm here, you know? I want to wake up and actually feel refreshed and ready to put on a pot of coffee, instead of dreading how heavy my legs are going to feel when I swing them over the edge of the mattress. But as far as actually wishing I wouldn't wake up, or thinking life isn't worth it? No, doctor, I've never had a thought like that. I just want to get back to being my normal self.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had periods where you felt unusually energetic, unusually confident, or needed less sleep than usual for several days in a row?\n\nPatient: Oh, goodness, no. The dates are coming up and I just can't muster up an ounce of enthusiasm to pick up the phone or plan a menu. It's like all the color has just drained right out of my life, and all these things that used to fill up my days and make me happy just seem like... like mountains I can't climb. I just don't care anymore, and I don't understand where all that energy went.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling down or sad most of the time, even when there's no particular reason for it?\n\nPatient: Well, yes, I suppose I have. It's... it's like this constant weight, doctor. Every single morning, I wake up and before my feet even hit the floor, before I've even had a chance to think about what day it is or what I'm supposed to be doing, there's just this... this heavy, sad feeling sitting right on my chest. It's almost physical, the way it presses down on me. I'll shuffle out to the kitchen, and my kitchen, it's this lovely bright yellow, you see. My husband painted it for me a few years ago because he knows how much I love the morning sun coming through the window over the sink. And I'll get the coffee going, just like I always do, listening to the pot gurgle and spit, but it doesn't matter. I can be standing right there in the sunlight, holding a warm mug, and I just feel like all the warmth has gone out of the world. It's just cold and gray inside my head. \n\nAnd there's no reason for it! That's the part that really gets to me, doctor. I have a good life. A wonderful husband, a nice home, we're not struggling to pay the bills or anything like that. I shouldn't be feeling this way. But most days, it's just there, this deep, unshakeable sadness that follows me from room to room. I'll sit in my armchair in the living room, looking out the window at the neighbor's garden, and I'll just start crying. For absolutely no reason at all. The tears just come, and I can't stop them, and I sit there wiping my face with a tissue, feeling so foolish because nothing bad has happened. It's just this pervasive gloom, this feeling that everything is just... sad. And it doesn't lift. It just lingers there, day in and day out, making everything feel so incredibly difficult and pointless. I just don't know how to shake it off, doctor. It's like I'm stuck under a dark cloud that won't move, no matter how hard the wind blows.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any significant changes in your appetite or weight lately, like losing or gaining a lot without trying?\n\nPatient: Well, like I was trying to explain a little bit earlier with the toast and the sweets, my eating has just gotten completely, entirely turned around. In fact, that's really one of the main things that made me realize I needed to come in and see you, because it's been going on for months now and I just can't seem to get a handle on it. \n\nIt's just so unpredictable. There are days, sometimes three or four days in a row, where I'll get up and the thought of putting a real meal together is just... well, it's impossible. I'll pour my coffee, and I'll put a single slice of plain white bread in the toaster, and I'll just sit at the kitchen table picking at it. I'll tear off a little corner, take a sip of coffee, and before I know it, it's three o'clock in the afternoon and that same piece of toast is still sitting there on my napkin, hard as a rock. I just have absolutely no desire for it. \n\nBut then, the very next week, it will swing in the exact opposite direction. I'll be doing my regular shopping down at the grocery store, and I'll just find myself standing there in the middle of the bakery aisle. I'll be staring at all those plastic containers on the tables—the muffins, the brownies, those soft sugar cookies with the thick layer of bright pink frosting and the little round sprinkles. And I'll buy them. I'll buy two or three packages at a time, bring them home, and just eat them one after another. And the most bizarre part of it all, doctor, is that I don't even really enjoy them! They just taste like pure sugar and shortening, not like a nice homemade treat at all, but I just keep eating them anyway. \n\nSo with all that back and forth, you can imagine what a mess it is. My appetite is just all over the place, and because of it, my clothes are fitting so strangely. One week the waistband on my favorite slacks is hanging loose, and the next week it's pinching me because of all those bakery cookies. It's just exhausting, having my body's routine so completely turned upside down for months on end like this.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed that you're feeling much more tired or that you're lacking energy for even simple tasks most days?\n\nPatient: Oh, heavens, yes. Yes, I am just... I am so bone-tired all the time. And it doesn't even make sense, because it's not like I'm out digging ditches or anything. It's just... well, my aunt lives with us, you see. We took her in a while back, and sometimes she needs things in the middle of the night. But even on the good nights—even after a full, completely uninterrupted night of sleep when my aunt doesn't call out for me once—I still wake up feeling just completely drained. \n\nI'll get up, and by the time breakfast is over, I feel completely worn down. And I'm not making a big spread, just, you know, getting something simple together, but I'll be standing there at the counter and I just feel this heavy exhaustion in my arms and my legs. It's like my battery is on empty before the day has even really started. \n\nAnd the simplest little chores... they just take so much more out of me than they ever should. The laundry, for instance. I have this white plastic laundry basket, the same one I've used for years, and just carrying it from the bedroom down the hall to the washing machine... I have to stop and catch my breath. It feels like I'm lugging a basket full of bricks. I'll just leave it sitting there in the hallway because I don't have the strength to sort the darks from the lights. \n\nEven just making phone calls! I know I told you earlier about avoiding the church ladies and the bowling, but even just picking up the receiver to pay a bill or, I don't know, check in on my sister... it just feels like this enormous, impossible hurdle. It takes so much energy just to hold a conversation. I just don't have the stamina for any of it anymore, doctor, and I just end up sitting in my chair feeling completely wiped out by ten in the morning. I used to be on my feet all day, and now just getting through a normal morning routine feels like I've run a marathon.\n\nDoctor: Have you been having trouble sleeping lately, like waking up in the middle of the night or early in the morning and not being able to go back to sleep, or on the flip side, do you find yourself sleeping much more than usual?\n\nPatient: Well, it's funny you should ask about sleep, doctor, because it's... well, it's not really an issue for me, not in the way you're describing. I mean, my sleep itself is mostly fine. I'm not tossing and turning all night, and I'm certainly not waking up at three in the morning staring at the ceiling, unable to drift back off. I know some people get like that, my neighbor Mrs. Higgins down the street, she tells me she's up half the night pacing the floor, but that's not me at all. \n\nWhen I go to bed, I sleep. And I'm not sleeping the day away either, you know? I don't stay in bed until noon or take long naps in the afternoon. I still get up at my regular time, right around six-thirty, just like I have for the last twenty years. It's just that, like I was saying before, even when I do sleep right through the night—even when my aunt is quiet and I get a solid eight hours—I still wake up feeling just completely exhausted. So the sleep itself seems normal, I'm just not getting any real rest from it, if that makes sense. But as far as actually falling asleep or staying asleep, or sleeping too much, no, I haven't noticed any changes there.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed that your movements have slowed down or that you're moving more sluggishly than usual, or on the flip side, that you're more restless or fidgety without being able to sit still?\n\nPatient: Well, it's definitely the first one. The slowing down. I'm certainly not bouncing off the walls or fidgeting, I just don't have the energy for that, like I was telling you before. But as for moving sluggishly... yes. It's actually gotten to the point where other people are pointing it out, which is just so embarrassing for me. \n\nJust the other day, I was at the grocery store—the Kroger over on Fifth Street, you know the one with the big floral department right when you walk in—and I was just trying to get my shopping done. And I felt like I was moving through thick mud. Every single step just took so much deliberate effort. And an old acquaintance of mine came up behind me in the produce section, and she actually stopped and asked me if my hip was bothering me! She said I was moving so slow and shuffling along so much that she thought I might be needing a hip replacement soon. And I didn't even know what to say to her, because my hips are perfectly fine! I've never had joint problems in my life, knock on wood, but I just couldn't seem to make my legs move any faster than a slow drag past the apples. \n\nAnd it's not just the walking, either. It's... well, it's like my brain and my mouth are moving through that same mud. I still go to these little community board meetings once a month—well, I try to, anyway, when I can muster the strength to get out the door—and someone will ask me the absolute simplest thing. They'll say, \"Did you remember to bring the sign-in sheet?\" or \"What do you think about the new parking rules?\" And I just sit there. I hear them perfectly fine, but it takes me just absolutely forever to gather my thoughts and actually push the words out of my mouth to answer them. It's like there's this long, terrible pause where everyone is just staring at me across the folding table, waiting, and I'm just trying to find the spark to form a basic sentence. So, yes, it's a very noticeable sluggishness, both in how I walk and how I talk, and people are definitely starting to ask questions about it because I'm just not my usual quick self at all.\n\nDoctor: Have you had repeated episodes of eating a lot of food in a short amount of time, even when you're not hungry, and felt like you couldn't stop yourself once you started?\n\nPatient: Oh, goodness, no. No, nothing like that at all. I mean, the idea of eating a lot of food right now, especially when I'm not hungry... it just sounds completely exhausting. Like I was saying before about having no energy, even just making a simple meal feels like climbing a mountain. So sitting down and eating a huge amount of food? I can't even imagine it. \n\nI know some people eat when they're upset or stressed. My sister-in-law, Martha, she's like that. Whenever she and my brother have a spat, she'll bake an entire batch of chocolate chip cookies and eat half of them before they've even cooled on the rack. She says it comforts her, but I just don't have that kind of relationship with food. \n\nHonestly, lately, I just kind of pick at things. I'll have a piece of toast in the morning, maybe a little soup for lunch, but I'm certainly not raiding the pantry or eating until I'm stuffed. I just don't have the appetite for it, and I definitely don't have those moments where I feel out of control with eating. It's more like I have to remind myself to eat just to keep my strength up, what little strength I have left, anyway. So, no, I haven't been doing anything like that at all.\n\nDoctor: Have you had any thoughts about life not being worth living, or found yourself wishing you wouldn't wake up in the morning?\n\nPatient: Oh, no. No, I don't think like that at all. I know I've been saying how exhausting everything is, and how just getting through the day feels like such a monumental chore, but I certainly don't want to stop living. I mean, my goodness, I still have things I need to do. I have my little garden out back—well, it's mostly just hostas and a few sad-looking tomato plants right now because I haven't had the energy to weed it properly, but I still like knowing it's there. And who would take care of my cat, Barnaby? He's such a fussy thing, he only eats the chicken pate, never the beef, and he gets so anxious if I'm not up by a certain time to open his tin. \n\nI remember years ago, my cousin Brenda went through a very dark time after the local plant closed down, and she used to say things exactly like that, about just wanting to go to sleep and never wake up. It used to just break my heart and scare me half to death to hear her talk that way. I'd sit with her on her porch for hours, sometimes until it got pitch black out, just trying to convince her that things would turn around and that she had so much left to give. So I know what that looks like, and I know how serious it is when someone starts talking like that, but that's just not where my head is at. \n\nEven with the sluggishness, and the absolute drain I feel every morning, I still want to be here. I just want to feel *better* while I'm here, you know? I want to wake up and actually feel refreshed and ready to put on a pot of coffee, instead of dreading how heavy my legs are going to feel when I swing them over the edge of the mattress. But as far as actually wishing I wouldn't wake up, or thinking life isn't worth it? No, doctor, I've never had a thought like that. I just want to get back to being my normal self.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had periods where you felt unusually energetic, unusually confident, or needed less sleep than usual for several days in a row?\n\nPatient: Oh, goodness, no. Unusually energetic? That sounds like a completely foreign concept to me right now, and honestly, even when I'm feeling like my regular self, I'm just a steady, middle-of-the-road sort of person. I've never been one of those people who just buzzes around with endless energy. \n\nI know the type you mean, though. My neighbor, Carol—she lives two houses down, the one with the bright yellow door that she painted herself last spring—she gets like that sometimes. She'll suddenly decide she needs to reorganize her entire garage, and she'll be out there at six in the morning, hauling boxes and sweeping out dust, and she'll just keep going until the sun goes down. And she'll talk your ear off the whole time if you happen to walk by, just so full of herself and whatever new project she's dreamed up. She always says she only needs four hours of sleep when she gets into one of her \"moods,\" as she calls them. \n\nBut me? No, never. I've always needed my solid eight hours of sleep, sometimes nine if it's been a long week. Even before I started feeling so bogged down and heavy like I am now, I was never one to skip sleep or feel like I could conquer the world on just a few hours' rest. If I don't get a good night's sleep, I'm just useless the next day. I get a headache right behind my eyes, and I can't focus on my crossword puzzles—I usually do the one in the Sunday paper, but I haven't even been able to manage that lately. \n\nAnd as for feeling unusually confident... well, I'm just a regular person. I don't think I'm better or more special than anyone else. I've never had a time where I felt like I was on top of the world or could do no wrong. I just try to live a quiet life, take care of Barnaby, keep my house tidy—when I have the energy, anyway—and mind my own business. So, no, I've never had any periods like what you're describing. Never. I just wish I could have a fraction of normal energy back, let alone an unusual amount of it.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Well, no, not really. I mean, I know I mentioned the crossword puzzles earlier, but that's really just because my eyes get tired and I just don't have the stamina to sit there holding the paper and staring at the tiny print. It's not that I can't figure out the clues or that my brain is fuzzy. My mind is as sharp as a tack, thank goodness. If I lost my mind on top of feeling this heavy and exhausted all the time, well, I just don't know what I'd do. 

Just the other day, I was sitting in my armchair—the green one by the front window, the fabric is actually starting to fray a bit on the left armrest, which I really need to get fixed eventually. Maybe I'll call that upholsterer downtown, Mr. Henderson, if he hasn't retired yet. Anyway, I was sitting there and I planned out my entire grocery list right in my head. I knew exactly what I needed: the chicken pate for Barnaby, of course, two cans of diced tomatoes, a loaf of rye bread, and that specific brand of dish soap with the aloe that doesn't dry out my hands. I didn't even write it down, and when Carol ran to the store for me, I rattled it off to her perfectly. So my memory and my thinking are just fine. 

And making decisions? No trouble there at all. I know exactly what needs to be done. Like with the household bills, I know precisely which ones are due on the 15th and which ones can wait until the end of the month, and I balance my checkbook down to the penny. The thinking part is perfectly easy. It's just... getting the physical energy to walk over to the desk, pull out the stamps, and actually write the checks. That's the part that feels like wading through molasses. 

My friend Evelyn, now, she has terrible trouble making decisions. We used to go to the diner on Main Street for lunch on Tuesdays, and she would stare at the menu for twenty minutes, flipping back and forth between the egg salad and the turkey club, driving the poor waitress absolutely up the wall. I've never been like that, and I'm still not. I know my own mind. I know what I want and what I think. It's just my body that refuses to cooperate with me right now. So, no, I wouldn't say I'm having any trouble focusing or thinking clearly. It's all up there, just waiting for the rest of me to catch up.

I'll spend the entire day just picking at a piece of dry toast, maybe nursing a cup of black coffee until it goes cold, and that's it. That's all I can manage. But then, out of nowhere, it swings the other way. I'll find myself wandering the aisles of the grocery store, and I'll just stand there in the bakery section, staring at all the cakes and cookies. And I buy them. I buy so many sweets, things I know I shouldn't even be eating, things I don't even really enjoy that much. I'll sit at my kitchen table and just eat them, one after another, and I don't even taste them half the time. It's just this... this compulsion, I guess you could call it. And it's so frustrating because I've always been fairly regular with my meals, you know? Breakfast, lunch, dinner, maybe a little snack in the afternoon. But now, it's just this chaotic mess, and I don't know how to fix it. It's like I have no control over my own body anymore.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself overly concerned about your weight or body shape, and have you been intentionally restricting the amount of food you eat on a regular basis?\n\nPatient: No, no, it's not like that at all. I mean, I'm not sitting here counting calories or obsessing over the scale, you know? I've never been one of those women who stands in front of the mirror pinching every little bit of flesh, worrying about fitting into a certain dress size. I'm 50 years old, for heaven's sake, I've lived in this body long enough to just let it be. And I'm certainly not starving myself on purpose. When I don't eat, when I just sit there with that dry toast I mentioned, it's not because I'm trying to lose weight or because I think I'm too heavy. It's just... the desire is gone. The physical sensation of hunger just completely vanishes, like someone flipped a switch in my brain and turned off the part that tells me I need sustenance. And then, when I do eat all those sweets, it's not a failure of some diet I'm on. It's just this strange, hollow feeling I'm trying to fill, even though I know a dozen cookies won't actually do it. So, no, I wouldn't say I'm concerned about my shape, not in the way you're asking. It's more about how disconnected I feel from the whole process of nourishing myself. It's just so bizarre, doctor, to have your body do these things without any real reason or plan behind it.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed that you don't enjoy things as much as you used to, or that you've lost interest in activities you once found pleasurable?\n\nPatient: Oh, yes. Yes, I have. It's actually funny you ask that, because my husband was just saying the other day that my sewing room has been awfully quiet lately. I used to... well, I used to get so excited about my quilting squares. I have these beautiful floral prints I bought on sale down at the fabric store, and I had this whole pattern mapped out for a double-wedding ring quilt. I used to lay them all out on the spare bed and just feel so much joy figuring out the color blocks and the borders. It would take up my whole afternoon. But now? I walk past that room, I look at all those supplies neatly stacked in their little clear plastic bins, and I just... I feel absolutely nothing. I can't make myself care the way I used to. \n\nAnd it's not just the quilting, doctor. It's really everything. Tuesday nights have been bowling night for me and a few of the ladies from the neighborhood for, oh, going on seven years now. We even had these silly matching shirts made a few years back. I used to look forward to it all week, just the chatter and the sound of the pins and getting out of the house, but the last few months... I just can't bring myself to go. They call and call, and I just sit there and stare at the phone ringing. I don't even care about my average slipping. \n\nAnd the church, too. I've always been the one in charge of planning the potlucks for the fellowship hall. You know how it is, if you don't organize it properly and stay on top of people, you end up with six macaroni salads, a dozen store-bought dinner rolls, and not a single hot main dish. I used to love organizing those sign-up sheets, calling people up, making sure everything was perfect and welcoming for the congregation. I took pride in it. But lately, I look at the church calendar hanging on my fridge, and it's just... it's just a piece of paper. The dates are coming up and I just can't muster up an ounce of enthusiasm to pick up the phone or plan a menu. It's like all the color has just drained right out of my life, and all these things that used to fill up my days and make me happy just seem like... like mountains I can't climb. I just don't care anymore, and I don't understand where all that energy went.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling down or sad most of the time, even when there's no particular reason for it?\n\nPatient: Well, yes, I suppose I have. It's... it's like this constant weight, doctor. Every single morning, I wake up and before my feet even hit the floor, before I've even had a chance to think about what day it is or what I'm supposed to be doing, there's just this... this heavy, sad feeling sitting right on my chest. It's almost physical, the way it presses down on me. I'll shuffle out to the kitchen, and my kitchen, it's this lovely bright yellow, you see. My husband painted it for me a few years ago because he knows how much I love the morning sun coming through the window over the sink. And I'll get the coffee going, just like I always do, listening to the pot gurgle and spit, but it doesn't matter. I can be standing right there in the sunlight, holding a warm mug, and I just feel like all the warmth has gone out of the world. It's just cold and gray inside my head. \n\nAnd there's no reason for it! That's the part that really gets to me, doctor. I have a good life. A wonderful husband, a nice home, we're not struggling to pay the bills or anything like that. I shouldn't be feeling this way. But most days, it's just there, this deep, unshakeable sadness that follows me from room to room. I'll sit in my armchair in the living room, looking out the window at the neighbor's garden, and I'll just start crying. For absolutely no reason at all. The tears just come, and I can't stop them, and I sit there wiping my face with a tissue, feeling so foolish because nothing bad has happened. It's just this pervasive gloom, this feeling that everything is just... sad. And it doesn't lift. It just lingers there, day in and day out, making everything feel so incredibly difficult and pointless. I just don't know how to shake it off, doctor. It's like I'm stuck under a dark cloud that won't move, no matter how hard the wind blows.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any significant changes in your appetite or weight lately, like losing or gaining a lot without trying?\n\nPatient: Well, like I was trying to explain a little bit earlier with the toast and the sweets, my eating has just gotten completely, entirely turned around. In fact, that's really one of the main things that made me realize I needed to come in and see you, because it's been going on for months now and I just can't seem to get a handle on it. \n\nIt's just so unpredictable. There are days, sometimes three or four days in a row, where I'll get up and the thought of putting a real meal together is just... well, it's impossible. I'll pour my coffee, and I'll put a single slice of plain white bread in the toaster, and I'll just sit at the kitchen table picking at it. I'll tear off a little corner, take a sip of coffee, and before I know it, it's three o'clock in the afternoon and that same piece of toast is still sitting there on my napkin, hard as a rock. I just have absolutely no desire for it. \n\nBut then, the very next week, it will swing in the exact opposite direction. I'll be doing my regular shopping down at the grocery store, and I'll just find myself standing there in the middle of the bakery aisle. I'll be staring at all those plastic containers on the tables—the muffins, the brownies, those soft sugar cookies with the thick layer of bright pink frosting and the little round sprinkles. And I'll buy them. I'll buy two or three packages at a time, bring them home, and just eat them one after another. And the most bizarre part of it all, doctor, is that I don't even really enjoy them! They just taste like pure sugar and shortening, not like a nice homemade treat at all, but I just keep eating them anyway. \n\nSo with all that back and forth, you can imagine what a mess it is. My appetite is just all over the place, and because of it, my clothes are fitting so strangely. One week the waistband on my favorite slacks is hanging loose, and the next week it's pinching me because of all those bakery cookies. It's just exhausting, having my body's routine so completely turned upside down for months on end like this.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed that you're feeling much more tired or that you're lacking energy for even simple tasks most days?\n\nPatient: Oh, heavens, yes. Yes, I am just... I am so bone-tired all the time. And it doesn't even make sense, because it's not like I'm out digging ditches or anything. It's just... well, my aunt lives with us, you see. We took her in a while back, and sometimes she needs things in the middle of the night. But even on the good nights—even after a full, completely uninterrupted night of sleep when my aunt doesn't call out for me once—I still wake up feeling just completely drained. \n\nI'll get up, and by the time breakfast is over, I feel completely worn down. And I'm not making a big spread, just, you know, getting something simple together, but I'll be standing there at the counter and I just feel this heavy exhaustion in my arms and my legs. It's like my battery is on empty before the day has even really started. \n\nAnd the simplest little chores... they just take so much more out of me than they ever should. The laundry, for instance. I have this white plastic laundry basket, the same one I've used for years, and just carrying it from the bedroom down the hall to the washing machine... I have to stop and catch my breath. It feels like I'm lugging a basket full of bricks. I'll just leave it sitting there in the hallway because I don't have the strength to sort the darks from the lights. \n\nEven just making phone calls! I know I told you earlier about avoiding the church ladies and the bowling, but even just picking up the receiver to pay a bill or, I don't know, check in on my sister... it just feels like this enormous, impossible hurdle. It takes so much energy just to hold a conversation. I just don't have the stamina for any of it anymore, doctor, and I just end up sitting in my chair feeling completely wiped out by ten in the morning. I used to be on my feet all day, and now just getting through a normal morning routine feels like I've run a marathon.\n\nDoctor: Have you been having trouble sleeping lately, like waking up in the middle of the night or early in the morning and not being able to go back to sleep, or on the flip side, do you find yourself sleeping much more than usual?\n\nPatient: Well, it's funny you should ask about sleep, doctor, because it's... well, it's not really an issue for me, not in the way you're describing. I mean, my sleep itself is mostly fine. I'm not tossing and turning all night, and I'm certainly not waking up at three in the morning staring at the ceiling, unable to drift back off. I know some people get like that, my neighbor Mrs. Higgins down the street, she tells me she's up half the night pacing the floor, but that's not me at all. \n\nWhen I go to bed, I sleep. And I'm not sleeping the day away either, you know? I don't stay in bed until noon or take long naps in the afternoon. I still get up at my regular time, right around six-thirty, just like I have for the last twenty years. It's just that, like I was saying before, even when I do sleep right through the night—even when my aunt is quiet and I get a solid eight hours—I still wake up feeling just completely exhausted. So the sleep itself seems normal, I'm just not getting any real rest from it, if that makes sense. But as far as actually falling asleep or staying asleep, or sleeping too much, no, I haven't noticed any changes there.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed that your movements have slowed down or that you're moving more sluggishly than usual, or on the flip side, that you're more restless or fidgety without being able to sit still?\n\nPatient: Well, it's definitely the first one. The slowing down. I'm certainly not bouncing off the walls or fidgeting, I just don't have the energy for that, like I was telling you before. But as for moving sluggishly... yes. It's actually gotten to the point where other people are pointing it out, which is just so embarrassing for me. \n\nJust the other day, I was at the grocery store—the Kroger over on Fifth Street, you know the one with the big floral department right when you walk in—and I was just trying to get my shopping done. And I felt like I was moving through thick mud. Every single step just took so much deliberate effort. And an old acquaintance of mine came up behind me in the produce section, and she actually stopped and asked me if my hip was bothering me! She said I was moving so slow and shuffling along so much that she thought I might be needing a hip replacement soon. And I didn't even know what to say to her, because my hips are perfectly fine! I've never had joint problems in my life, knock on wood, but I just couldn't seem to make my legs move any faster than a slow drag past the apples. \n\nAnd it's not just the walking, either. It's... well, it's like my brain and my mouth are moving through that same mud. I still go to these little community board meetings once a month—well, I try to, anyway, when I can muster the strength to get out the door—and someone will ask me the absolute simplest thing. They'll say, \"Did you remember to bring the sign-in sheet?\" or \"What do you think about the new parking rules?\" And I just sit there. I hear them perfectly fine, but it takes me just absolutely forever to gather my thoughts and actually push the words out of my mouth to answer them. It's like there's this long, terrible pause where everyone is just staring at me across the folding table, waiting, and I'm just trying to find the spark to form a basic sentence. So, yes, it's a very noticeable sluggishness, both in how I walk and how I talk, and people are definitely starting to ask questions about it because I'm just not my usual quick self at all.\n\nDoctor: Have you had repeated episodes of eating a lot of food in a short amount of time, even when you're not hungry, and felt like you couldn't stop yourself once you started?\n\nPatient: Oh, goodness, no. No, nothing like that at all. I mean, the idea of eating a lot of food right now, especially when I'm not hungry... it just sounds completely exhausting. Like I was saying before about having no energy, even just making a simple meal feels like climbing a mountain. So sitting down and eating a huge amount of food? I can't even imagine it. \n\nI know some people eat when they're upset or stressed. My sister-in-law, Martha, she's like that. Whenever she and my brother have a spat, she'll bake an entire batch of chocolate chip cookies and eat half of them before they've even cooled on the rack. She says it comforts her, but I just don't have that kind of relationship with food. \n\nHonestly, lately, I just kind of pick at things. I'll have a piece of toast in the morning, maybe a little soup for lunch, but I'm certainly not raiding the pantry or eating until I'm stuffed. I just don't have the appetite for it, and I definitely don't have those moments where I feel out of control with eating. It's more like I have to remind myself to eat just to keep my strength up, what little strength I have left, anyway. So, no, I haven't been doing anything like that at all.\n\nDoctor: Have you had any thoughts about life not being worth living, or found yourself wishing you wouldn't wake up in the morning?\n\nPatient: Oh, no. No, I don't think like that at all. I know I've been saying how exhausting everything is, and how just getting through the day feels like such a monumental chore, but I certainly don't want to stop living. I mean, my goodness, I still have things I need to do. I have my little garden out back—well, it's mostly just hostas and a few sad-looking tomato plants right now because I haven't had the energy to weed it properly, but I still like knowing it's there. And who would take care of my cat, Barnaby? He's such a fussy thing, he only eats the chicken pate, never the beef, and he gets so anxious if I'm not up by a certain time to open his tin. \n\nI remember years ago, my cousin Brenda went through a very dark time after the local plant closed down, and she used to say things exactly like that, about just wanting to go to sleep and never wake up. It used to just break my heart and scare me half to death to hear her talk that way. I'd sit with her on her porch for hours, sometimes until it got pitch black out, just trying to convince her that things would turn around and that she had so much left to give. So I know what that looks like, and I know how serious it is when someone starts talking like that, but that's just not where my head is at. \n\nEven with the sluggishness, and the absolute drain I feel every morning, I still want to be here. I just want to feel *better* while I'm here, you know? I want to wake up and actually feel refreshed and ready to put on a pot of coffee, instead of dreading how heavy my legs are going to feel when I swing them over the edge of the mattress. But as far as actually wishing I wouldn't wake up, or thinking life isn't worth it? No, doctor, I've never had a thought like that. I just want to get back to being my normal self.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had periods where you felt unusually energetic, unusually confident, or needed less sleep than usual for several days in a row?\n\nPatient: Oh, goodness, no. Unusually energetic? That sounds like a completely foreign concept to me right now, and honestly, even when I'm feeling like my regular self, I'm just a steady, middle-of-the-road sort of person. I've never been one of those people who just buzzes around with endless energy. \n\nI know the type you mean, though. My neighbor, Carol—she lives two houses down, the one with the bright yellow door that she painted herself last spring—she gets like that sometimes. She'll suddenly decide she needs to reorganize her entire garage, and she'll be out there at six in the morning, hauling boxes and sweeping out dust, and she'll just keep going until the sun goes down. And she'll talk your ear off the whole time if you happen to walk by, just so full of herself and whatever new project she's dreamed up. She always says she only needs four hours of sleep when she gets into one of her \"moods,\" as she calls them. \n\nBut me? No, never. I've always needed my solid eight hours of sleep, sometimes nine if it's been a long week. Even before I started feeling so bogged down and heavy like I am now, I was never one to skip sleep or feel like I could conquer the world on just a few hours' rest. If I don't get a good night's sleep, I'm just useless the next day. I get a headache right behind my eyes, and I can't focus on my crossword puzzles—I usually do the one in the Sunday paper, but I haven't even been able to manage that lately. \n\nAnd as for feeling unusually confident... well, I'm just a regular person. I don't think I'm better or more special than anyone else. I've never had a time where I felt like I was on top of the world or could do no wrong. I just try to live a quiet life, take care of Barnaby, keep my house tidy—when I have the energy, anyway—and mind my own business. So, no, I've never had any periods like what you're describing. Never. I just wish I could have a fraction of normal energy back, let alone an unusual amount of it.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed that it's harder to focus, make decisions, or think clearly lately?\n\nPatient: Well, no, not really. I mean, I know I mentioned the crossword puzzles earlier, but that's really just because my eyes get tired and I just don't have the stamina to sit there holding the paper and staring at the tiny print. It's not that I can't figure out the clues or that my brain is fuzzy. My mind is as sharp as a tack, thank goodness. If I lost my mind on top of feeling this heavy and exhausted all the time, well, I just don't know what I'd do. \n\nJust the other day, I was sitting in my armchair—the green one by the front window, the fabric is actually starting to fray a bit on the left armrest, which I really need to get fixed eventually. Maybe I'll call that upholsterer downtown, Mr. Henderson, if he hasn't retired yet. Anyway, I was sitting there and I planned out my entire grocery list right in my head. I knew exactly what I needed: the chicken pate for Barnaby, of course, two cans of diced tomatoes, a loaf of rye bread, and that specific brand of dish soap with the aloe that doesn't dry out my hands. I didn't even write it down, and when Carol ran to the store for me, I rattled it off to her perfectly. So my memory and my thinking are just fine. \n\nAnd making decisions? No trouble there at all. I know exactly what needs to be done. Like with the household bills, I know precisely which ones are due on the 15th and which ones can wait until the end of the month, and I balance my checkbook down to the penny. The thinking part is perfectly easy. It's just... getting the physical energy to walk over to the desk, pull out the stamps, and actually write the checks. That's the part that feels like wading through molasses. \n\nMy friend Evelyn, now, she has terrible trouble making decisions. We used to go to the diner on Main Street for lunch on Tuesdays, and she would stare at the menu for twenty minutes, flipping back and forth between the egg salad and the turkey club, driving the poor waitress absolutely up the wall. I've never been like that, and I'm still not. I know my own mind. I know what I want and what I think. It's just my body that refuses to cooperate with me right now. So, no, I wouldn't say I'm having any trouble focusing or thinking clearly. It's all up there, just waiting for the rest of me to catch up.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. Two weeks? Oh, my, I wish it had only been two weeks. No, doctor, it’s been much, much longer than that. It’s been going on for about seven months now, every single day. 

I know the timeline exactly because I can trace it right back to when my aunt came to live with me after her fall. She was just walking down her front steps to get the mail—she has one of those old, heavy metal mailboxes right by the street, the kind the snowplows always seem to dent in the winter—and she slipped on some wet leaves and took a terrible tumble. Fractured her hip, poor thing. She’s in her late seventies, so it was quite a scare for all of us. So, of course, she couldn't stay in her house alone, and she had to move into my spare bedroom. You know, the one at the end of the hall where I keep my sewing machine and all my extra linens. 

That was seven months ago, and I can tell you, every single day since then has just been a complete struggle to find any energy or joy in things. I used to love having company, but ever since she moved in, I just wake up feeling so bogged down and sad, and it hasn't let up for a single afternoon. I have to make her breakfast every morning—she insists on steel-cut oats, which take simply forever to simmer on the stove compared to the instant kind—and I just stand there at the burner stirring the pot, feeling like I have lead in my shoes, just completely drained of any interest in the day ahead. So, yes, it's been every day, but for seven solid months, not just two weeks.

I have my little garden out back—well, it's mostly just hostas and a few sad-looking tomato plants right now because I haven't had the energy to weed it properly, but I still like knowing it's there. And who would take care of my cat, Barnaby? He's such a fussy thing, he only eats the chicken pate, never the beef, and he gets so anxious if I'm not up by a certain time to open his tin. \n\nI remember years ago, my cousin Brenda went through a very dark time after the local plant closed down, and she used to say things exactly like that, about just wanting to go to sleep and never wake up. It used to just break my heart and scare me half to death to hear her talk that way. I'd sit with her on her porch for hours, sometimes until it got pitch black out, just trying to convince her that things would turn around and that she had so much left to give. So I know what that looks like, and I know how serious it is when someone starts talking like that, but that's just not where my head is at. \n\nEven with the sluggishness, and the absolute drain I feel every morning, I still want to be here. I just want to feel *better* while I'm here, you know? I want to wake up and actually feel refreshed and ready to put on a pot of coffee, instead of dreading how heavy my legs are going to feel when I swing them over the edge of the mattress. But as far as actually wishing I wouldn't wake up, or thinking life isn't worth it? No, doctor, I've never had a thought like that. I just want to get back to being my normal self.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had periods where you felt unusually energetic, unusually confident, or needed less sleep than usual for several days in a row?\n\nPatient: Oh, goodness, no. Unusually energetic? That sounds like a completely foreign concept to me right now, and honestly, even when I'm feeling like my regular self, I'm just a steady, middle-of-the-road sort of person. I've never been one of those people who just buzzes around with endless energy. \n\nI know the type you mean, though. My neighbor, Carol—she lives two houses down, the one with the bright yellow door that she painted herself last spring—she gets like that sometimes. She'll suddenly decide she needs to reorganize her entire garage, and she'll be out there at six in the morning, hauling boxes and sweeping out dust, and she'll just keep going until the sun goes down. And she'll talk your ear off the whole time if you happen to walk by, just so full of herself and whatever new project she's dreamed up. She always says she only needs four hours of sleep when she gets into one of her \"moods,\" as she calls them. \n\nBut me? No, never. I've always needed my solid eight hours of sleep, sometimes nine if it's been a long week. Even before I started feeling so bogged down and heavy like I am now, I was never one to skip sleep or feel like I could conquer the world on just a few hours' rest. If I don't get a good night's sleep, I'm just useless the next day. I get a headache right behind my eyes, and I can't focus on my crossword puzzles—I usually do the one in the Sunday paper, but I haven't even been able to manage that lately. \n\nAnd as for feeling unusually confident... well, I'm just a regular person. I don't think I'm better or more special than anyone else. I've never had a time where I felt like I was on top of the world or could do no wrong. I just try to live a quiet life, take care of Barnaby, keep my house tidy—when I have the energy, anyway—and mind my own business. So, no, I've never had any periods like what you're describing. Never. I just wish I could have a fraction of normal energy back, let alone an unusual amount of it.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed that it's harder to focus, make decisions, or think clearly lately?\n\nPatient: Well, no, not really. I mean, I know I mentioned the crossword puzzles earlier, but that's really just because my eyes get tired and I just don't have the stamina to sit there holding the paper and staring at the tiny print. It's not that I can't figure out the clues or that my brain is fuzzy. My mind is as sharp as a tack, thank goodness. If I lost my mind on top of feeling this heavy and exhausted all the time, well, I just don't know what I'd do. \n\nJust the other day, I was sitting in my armchair—the green one by the front window, the fabric is actually starting to fray a bit on the left armrest, which I really need to get fixed eventually. Maybe I'll call that upholsterer downtown, Mr. Henderson, if he hasn't retired yet. Anyway, I was sitting there and I planned out my entire grocery list right in my head. I knew exactly what I needed: the chicken pate for Barnaby, of course, two cans of diced tomatoes, a loaf of rye bread, and that specific brand of dish soap with the aloe that doesn't dry out my hands. I didn't even write it down, and when Carol ran to the store for me, I rattled it off to her perfectly. So my memory and my thinking are just fine. \n\nAnd making decisions? No trouble there at all. Like with the household bills, I know precisely which ones are due on the 15th and which ones can wait until the end of the month, and I balance my checkbook down to the penny. The thinking part is perfectly easy. It's just... getting the physical energy to walk over to the desk, pull out the stamps, and actually write the checks. That's the part that feels like wading through molasses. \n\nMy friend Evelyn, now, she has terrible trouble making decisions. We used to go to the diner on Main Street for lunch on Tuesdays, and she would stare at the menu for twenty minutes, flipping back and forth between the egg salad and the turkey club, driving the poor waitress absolutely up the wall. I've never been like that, and I'm still not. I know my own mind. I know what I want and what I think. It's just my body that refuses to cooperate with me right now. So, no, I wouldn't say I'm having any trouble focusing or thinking clearly. It's all up there, just waiting for the rest of me to catch up.\n\nDoctor: Have you had these feelings of sadness, loss of interest, and low energy every day for at least two weeks in a row?\n\nPatient: Two weeks? She was just walking down her front steps to get the mail—she has one of those old, heavy metal mailboxes right by the street, the kind the snowplows always seem to dent in the winter—and she slipped on some wet leaves and took a terrible tumble. Fractured her hip, poor thing. She’s in her late seventies, so it was quite a scare for all of us. So, of course, she couldn't stay in her house alone, and she had to move into my spare bedroom. You know, the one at the end of the hall where I keep my sewing machine and all my extra linens. \n\nThat was seven months ago, and I can tell you, every single day since then has just been a complete struggle to find any energy or joy in things. I used to love having company, but ever since she moved in, I just wake up feeling so bogged down and sad, and it hasn't let up for a single afternoon. I have to make her breakfast every morning—she insists on steel-cut oats, which take simply forever to simmer on the stove compared to the instant kind—and I just stand there at the burner stirring the pot, feeling like I have lead in my shoes, just completely drained of any interest in the day ahead. So, yes, it's been every day, but for seven solid months, not just two weeks.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
No, doctor, never. This is entirely new for me, which I suppose is part of why it’s been so terribly hard to cope with. I’ve always been such an even-keeled person, you know? Just very steady. I mean, of course, everyone has a bad day here and there. Like back in, oh, it must have been 2015 or maybe 2016, when the city was doing all that dreadful sewer work on our street. The noise was just incessant, from seven in the morning until five in the evening, with those massive trucks backing up and beeping, and the dust got all over my front porch furniture. I had just bought these lovely floral cushions for the wicker chairs, too. I was quite grumpy about it, naturally, and maybe a little down because I couldn't sit outside with my morning tea, but I never lost interest in my actual life. 

Even with all that racket, I still wanted to do my sewing, I still kept the house spotless, and I still made my banana bread every Sunday. I use a recipe with a dollop of sour cream in the batter, which is the real secret to keeping it moist, if you ever try baking it yourself. My point is, even when things were bothersome or stressful, I just pushed right through. I would just close the windows, turn on a fan for some white noise, and get on with my day. 

I have never had anything like this, where the weeks just blur together into months and I feel this... this awful, persistent heaviness. I've never had a time in my life where I couldn't bring myself to care about the things I usually love. Before my aunt moved in and all this started seven months ago, I was perfectly content. I had my routines, I had Barnaby to look after, I had my little visits with Carol and Evelyn. I’ve just never been the sort of person to stay down in the dumps for more than a day or two. So no, nothing like this has ever happened to me before. Not once in my fifty years. It's completely uncharted territory for me, and frankly, I just want to find my way back out of it.
